<note>The emblem described on this page features the "pelican in her piety," a potent symbol in both Christian iconography and alchemy. In an alchemical context, the pelican piercing its breast to feed its young with blood represents the <term>Rubedo</term> (the "Red Work"), symbolizing the final stage of the Great Work where the soul or substance is nourished and perfected through sacrifice and revitalization. The presence of the four roses below the pelican often suggests a Rosicrucian influence, a secret society focused on spiritual and physical transformation.</note>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>The text transitions from the formal title page of Brockes' spiritual poetry to the preface of a work documenting the "secret history" of an individual involved in the Rosicrucian Order. # Preface.

Since I made the decision to write the secret history of the upright Cedrinus, <note>Cedrinus is a fraternal name or pseudonym, a common practice in 18th-century secret societies to protect members' identities and signify their spiritual rank.</note> I have spared no effort, above all else, to get to know the man himself as intimately as possible. He is of an open character. One gets to know him quickly. But the history of a man's spirit is not so quickly learned; and this was my study. I have seen the man in manifold situations. I have observed him in joy and in sorrow. I am proud to call him my friend.

What Cedrinus did, in deciding to make known his secret history within such an order as that of the Gold and Rosy Cross <note>The Order of the Gold and Rosy Cross (Gold- und Rosenkreuzer) was an influential 18th-century German secret society that blended alchemy, mysticism, and traditional Lutheranism.</note>—to make it known according to the strict truth, without sparing his own weaknesses—truly, that is no ordinary thing!

How much was required for this decision, those may judge who know secret societies of that kind from their own experience.

<summary>This preface introduces the biography of a man known as Cedrinus, focusing on his spiritual development and his membership in the secret Order of the Gold and Rosy Cross.</summary>
<keywords>Cedrinus, secret history, Rosicrucians, Gold and Rosy Cross, secret societies, spiritual biography, Enlightenment-era mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by H. C. Albrecht. While the previous page established the author’s relationship with "Cedrinus" and his involvement in the Rosicrucian Order, this concluding section shifts to the author's role as the custodian of Cedrinus's private papers and issues a stark warning about the dangers of clandestine organizations.</meta>

### Preface

He has entrusted his papers to my faithful hands. I will preserve them, and in due time deposit them in a public library. There is still much within them that can be of use to our contemporaries and descendants. — I did not wish to have the original letters of the persons involved in this history printed. <note>In the 18th century, "printing letters" often caused public scandal, and Albrecht is likely protecting the identities of other high-ranking members of secret orders.</note> — I shall carefully observe how many a person behaves toward Cedrinus.

I still had much to say to the public in this preface; but I must break off. At this very moment, Cedrinus lies dangerously ill. Perhaps he will not live to see his own history fully in print. — Oh! Honest man, wherever you live, whatever you do: beware of societies that shun the light! <note>original: "Gesellschaften, die das Licht scheuen." This phrase was a common Enlightenment-era critique of secret societies (like the Rosicrucians or the Illuminati), suggesting their secrecy was a cover for superstition or political manipulation rather than enlightenment.</note>

Hamburg in May
1792.

->H. C. Albrecht.<-

<summary>H. C. Albrecht concludes his preface by announcing his custody of Cedrinus's private archive and issuing a final, urgent warning against the dangers of secret societies as Cedrinus faces a terminal illness.</summary>
<keywords>H. C. Albrecht, Cedrinus, secret societies, archival preservation, Enlightenment, Hamburg, 1792, deathbed warnings</keywords>

# Preface

He has entrusted his papers to my faithful hands. I shall preserve them and, in due time, deposit them in a public library. There is much within them that can be of use to our contemporaries and to posterity. — I have chosen not to include the original handwritten letters of the persons involved in this history. — I shall carefully observe how many a person deports himself toward **Cedrinus**. <note>"Cedrinus" is a pseudonym used to protect the identity of the individual involved. In the 18th century, such "key novels" or "secret histories" often used botanical or classical names for real people to avoid legal or social repercussions.</note>

I had much more to say to the Public in this preface; but I must break off. At this very moment, **Cedrinus** lies dangerously ill. Perhaps he will not live to see his own history fully in print. — Oh! Honest man, wherever you live, whatever you do: beware of societies that shun the light! <note>The phrase "societies that shun the light" (lichtscheue Gesellschaften) was a common 18th-century polemic against secret orders like the Rosicrucians or the Illuminati, suggesting their secrecy hid subversive or irrational goals.</note>

->Hamburg in May<-
->1792.<-

->**H. C. Albrecht.**<- <note>Heinrich Christoph Albrecht (1763–1800) was a German writer and linguist known for his critical views on the political and social influence of secret societies during the Enlightenment.</note>

<summary>In this preface, editor H. C. Albrecht explains how he came to possess the papers of "Cedrinus" and offers a stern warning against the dangers of secret societies. He frames the following text as a cautionary true story of a man currently on his deathbed.</summary>
<keywords>H. C. Albrecht, Cedrinus, secret societies, Hamburg, 18th-century literature, Enlightenment, pseudonyms, cautionary tale</keywords>

<term>Secret History</term> <note>original: "Geheime Geschichte". In the 18th century, a "Secret History" was a popular literary genre. These books purported to reveal the hidden, private, or scandalous truths behind public institutions and secretive organizations like the Freemasons or Rosicrucians.</note>, <term>Rosicrucian</term> <note>original: "Rosenkreuzer". A member of a legendary spiritual and cultural movement that arose in the early 17th century. By the time of this manuscript, it likely refers to the "Order of the Gold and Rosy Cross," a highly secretive and ritualistic society that blended alchemy, mysticism, and conservative politics.</note>, <term>First Book</term> <note>original: "Erstes Buch".</note>

--- Page 19 ---

## First Chapter.

### Initiation into Freemasonry. — Heightened Expectations.

A secret society derives its greatest advantages from the very circumstance that, in all probability, should be most detrimental to it. Who would want to hide without cause? And so stubbornly! This striking circumstance usually dictates judgments about secret societies more than the judge themselves realizes. It prompts them to reflect on a matter for which they entirely lack the knowledge and the means to obtain it. Thus, they imperceptibly form prejudices while believing they are judging with great composure.

Among all those who become <term>Freemasons</term> <note>original: "Freymaurer"</note>, it is likely precisely those who take this step with the greatest deliberation who have been most misled by some prejudice—one which, in earlier years and under favorable circumstances, the acquaintance with worthy men,

<vocab>First Chapter, Initiation, Freemason, Expectations, Secret Society, Prejudices</vocab>

<summary>This opening chapter explores the paradox of secret societies, noting that their secrecy creates a public fascination and a set of prejudices that often motivate people to join. The author suggests that even the most thoughtful initiates are influenced by positive impressions of the "worthy men" they encountered in their youth.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, secret societies, initiation, social psychology, prejudice, public perception, motives for joining</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, completing the thought regarding the acquaintance with worthy men ("kennen zu lernen"). The narrative transition links the general observation about secret societies to the specific biography of the protagonist.</meta>

...to know, who were Freemasons, and which then works all the more powerfully because they are not in a position to judge its truth or falsehood completely, yet believe they see probability before them.

The man whose secret history our readers will learn in the following pages only became a <term>Freemason</term> <note>original: "Freymaurer"</note> in the year 1779; and his admission was the fulfillment of a desire that a <note>The word used here is "Vorurtheil" (prejudice). In the 18th century, this often referred to a preconceived opinion or "pre-judgment" that could be either positive or negative.</note> prejudice had instilled in him ten years earlier. He was now thirty-three years old and a widower; and he had formed his first charming impressions of Freemasonry as a youth of twenty-one, in another place and in a different situation. He was admitted in Hamburg; and he had made his first acquaintance with Freemasons in Berlin.

These were upright people, helpful, obliging, and charitable; ready to seize every opportunity for good deeds and to make use of them even with great effort. They avoided notoriety instead of seeking it. They acted not so much on sudden impulses and the inspirations of the moment, but with more deliberation and awareness than the common mass of men usually demonstrate. Their respect for the laws of morality was not merely a consequence of devotion, but—

<summary>This page introduces the protagonist of the narrative, a man who joined the Freemasons in Hamburg in 1779 after a decade of admiring the society. It describes his early positive impressions of Masons in Berlin, characterizing them as exceptionally deliberate, charitable, and morally upright individuals.</summary>
<keywords>Hamburg, Berlin, 1779, Freemasonry, character traits, moral philosophy, biography, recruitment motives</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 4, which described the character of the Masons the protagonist met in Berlin. It completes the thought that their morality was not just based on devotion, but on a specific harmony of mind and heart.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>

rather it seemed to be grounded in a <term>way of thinking</term> <note>original: "Denkungsart"</note> in
which the activity of the intellect unites with the feelings of the
heart, in order to bring that pure harmony into life,
which pleases through its <note>The phrase "noble simplicity" (edle Einfalt) was a central aesthetic and moral ideal of the German Enlightenment, suggesting beauty and virtue that are unpretentious and natural.</note> noble simplicity,
without blinding through any sort of brilliance. One did not
admire them; but one loved them. One was not
carried away; but one felt deeply at ease
in their company. They did not speak enticingly of the <term>Order</term> <note>The Masonic Order.</note>; but
they also did not affect to hide that they were
<term>Freemasons</term>. They spoke of the nature of the
society with approval and respect, and of its
purpose with that elevated liveliness, which ennobles
all thoughts and sensations based on love,
and imparts a gentle enthusiasm to their expressions,
whereby at the same time the <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "Phantasie"; in 18th-century psychology, this was the faculty of the mind that processed and stored sensory images and feelings.</note> is set
into activity, while only the heart seems to
open itself.

Every elevated sensation of the <term>social virtues</term> <note>original: "geselligen Tugenden"; these were the interpersonal ethics—such as friendliness, empathy, and honesty—that were considered essential for a refined civil society.</note>
is in itself as soul-satisfying as it is charming.
It excites no desires while it grants
enjoyment. It places man in a situation
where nothing strains his attention or demands
his reflection. He makes no observations,
because he enjoys immediately, and the imagination
gains material for future times, in order
to [recall] a moment of pure pleasure in
memory again...

<page-num>6</page-num>

to provide. A society that grants or promises its members even a few minutes of such a beneficial elevation of the feeling of sociability—of the purest enjoyment of <term>humanity</term> <note>original: "Menschheit"; in this era, the term referred to the ideal of refined human nature and universal brotherhood.</note>—must inevitably gain charm for unspoiled people. However, it must never fall into the temptation of trying to bind this delicate bond with chains that seem stronger only because they are coarser.

This is such a simple truth that it is not even necessary to state it before a mistake is made that makes doing so necessary and—at the same time—useless. Into the image we create for ourselves of such a society, an unspoiled <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "Phantasie"</note> will naturally not introduce any feature that could disfigure the delicate beauty of the whole or destroy the fine proportion of the parts. We will not expect to find any of those <term>passions</term> <note>In the 18th century, "passions" (Leidenschaften) were intense emotions or desires that could drive great actions but were often seen as forces needing to be tempered by reason and sociability.</note> there which calm life by filling it with activity—passions which prompt great deeds, but never become amiable unless they submit to the beneficial superiority of those noble forces that must be solely active in a society which aims to ennoble the feelings of humanity.

Much was written about <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note> around this time; no longer so much in France, but all the more in Germany. Cedrinus <note>The pseudonym for the protagonist, likely based on the author's own experiences.</note> read most of it and found only that Freemasonry would be a very incomprehensible thing if one were to try to understand it from these writings. He believed that whoever was a Freemason must understand what was incomprehensible to him; for he could not imagine that people would write books that no one was meant to understand, or that people would read books they could not understand. The Freemasons did not enlighten him on this. One might smile kindly, with a sly expression and not without self-complacency, when Cedrinus let it be known that he could not understand what was incomprehensible. **Cedrinus** had not yet finished acquiring those branches of knowledge called school studies <note>original: "Schulstudia"; referring to the standard classical curriculum of the time.</note>, and was not familiar enough with ancient and medieval history to recognize, from certain characteristic expressions, the era in which the language was used or the class of people among whom it was current. Domestic and family circumstances stood in his way when he wished to be initiated as a <term>Freemason</term> <note>original: "Freymaurer"</note> immediately, even though a very accommodating opportunity presented itself at the time. For people had, in part, very unfavorable ideas about the Freemasons; and the fault did not lie so entirely with the profanes <note>original: "Profanen"; in Masonic terminology, this refers to the uninitiated or the general public.</note> as has often been blamed upon them. — Hamburg is a special place in several respects, and is particularly distinguished by a certain peculiarity in the way of thinking of its inhabitants, by virtue of which they receive any prejudice that does not enter into a very friendly relationship with their senses only with the greatest indifference. Many circumstances changed; and toward the end of the year 1778, no unfavorable relationship any longer prevented **Cedrinus** from offering himself in the usual manner as a candidate for <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note>. He revealed his wish to a trusted friend, who then informed him in February 1779 that he had proposed him to his **Lodge** <note>original: "Loge"</note> and would soon let him know the day of his initiation <note>original: "Aufnahme"; literally "reception" into the order.</note>.

It took place in that same month. The gathering was numerous; and the Lodge was one of those which had at that time submitted to the system of <term>Strict Observance</term> <note>original: "stricte Observanz"; a high-degree Masonic system popular in Germany during the 1760s and 70s, which claimed a secret connection to the medieval Knights Templar and required strict obedience to unknown superiors.</note>.

The situation into which the ceremonies of initiation place a prospective Freemason depends on the ideas he has previously formed of the matter, on his knowledge, on the vividness of his imagination, and especially on his receptivity to the solemn <note>original: "Feyerliche"; referring to the ritualistic gravity and sacred atmosphere of the ceremony.</note>.

If he combines wit and acumen with knowledge, and remains cold toward the deceptive qualities inherent in the solemnity: then this evening will be for him the beginning of an epoch of new observations, and he sees here a side of humanity that has never before been brought before his eyes. He has the opportunity to observe himself in a mood whose correct judgment depends only on his

Cedrinus was no <term>dialectician</term> <note>A person skilled in the art of logical argument and investigation.</note>, and had never occupied himself with logical or metaphysical investigations into the nature of truth. He possessed no more profound concepts of experience, communication, instruction, or representation than a man of sound common sense usually acquires in the school of ordinary life. But his imagination was also not of the flighty kind, and his soul did not belong at all to the class of those who—according to a certain mysterious theory of the holy <term>Gnosis</term> <note>original: "Gnosis"; from the Greek word for "knowledge," referring here to esoteric spiritual insight or secret wisdom claimed by various mystical groups.</note>—are created from a completely incomprehensible and divine element, in which pure fire and heavenly light are joined in a mystical way. What he saw and heard did not recommend itself much through clarity, and his previous reading was to blame for the fact that it did not even possess the full charm of novelty for him. — Nevertheless, this was only the beginning of the entire career that opened a prospect to him which was not limited, even if it shrouded objects in an opaque veil—one that, because it could be drawn aside, served as a shifting horizon. — He now had, at least, conditional promises to learn more in the future. — In some Masonic writings and speeches, curiosity

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page, completing the word "curiosity" (Neugierde). It follows Cedrinus's progression through the early degrees of Freemasonry.</meta>

curiosity among the reproachable emotions, and warns the members of the Order against it. Not to become curious when one is being made curious is perhaps a perfection of patience, for which only those exercises can prepare a person which the <term>profane world</term> <note>In Masonic terminology, "profane" refers to anything or anyone outside the brotherhood or the temple.</note> provides him no opportunity to undertake. — Cedrinus became more curious than before. He did not yet praise this day as "thrice happy." But since he was told that he would eventually do so, he would have liked to know when this time might come.

It was then the custom in the lodges of the <term>Strict Observance</term> <note>original: "stricte Observanz"; a Rite of Freemasonry founded in Germany in the mid-18th century, claiming descent from the Knights Templar.</note> that a man must have been a member of the first degree for one year, the second for two, and the third for three, before he could make further progress in so-called <term>Freemasonry</term> (as they used to express it). All those who did not know that the Strict Observance was not yet old themselves held this custom to be ancient. And only an immediate dispensation from the <term>Old Scottish Lodge</term> <note>A higher-tier governing body within the Strict Observance system that managed the "Scottish Master" degree.</note> could relieve someone, whose drive for knowledge became too powerful, of this vexatious waiting period. Cedrinus had to resign himself to it. He visited every lodge, heard the same expressions repeated very often, saw the same familiar objects, and participated in the same rituals.

In his first <term>year of apprenticeship</term> <note>original: "Lehrjahre"; in Freemasonry, this refers to the period a member spends in the first degree as an Entered Apprentice.</note>, he once visited Brother Sch\*\*, a kind-hearted, honest man, but one very limited in knowledge and intellectual power, who had already traveled through more than a few of life's more serious years and was a zealous Mason. Between two brothers of the Order, the conversation naturally turned easily to Freemasonry; and the young brother, following his innate frankness, forgot the restrictive status of the still meager and cautiously meted out instruction in which he found himself. He passed judgment on what he had experienced; he judged freely and expressed that he had expected more and hoped to learn more. —

Brother Sch\*\* was sufficiently interested by what he had experienced in the Order—all of which had been equally novel to him, and which his literary education was insufficient to survey or properly classify—that a refreshed memory of it lent his imagination a certain liveliness. This gave his eloquence, even though he only knew how to use words with imperfect skill, a certain effective and emphatic superiority, which is recognized everywhere as being very powerful under the name of <term>heartiness</term> <note>original: "Herzlichkeit"; implies a sincere, warm, and plain-spoken manner that carries emotional weight regardless of intellectual depth.</note>. He began to praise the importance and the great secrets of the Order more than he could substantiate them. He spoke according to his

Who likes to contradict someone who asserts even something strange with complete, heartfelt conviction? It was not so much the assurances of the well-meaning Brother Sch**—who cited the authority of a distant Prince, whom Cedrinus had not had the honor of knowing closely, to support his claims—but rather his own curiosity that drove him to accept the second <term>degree</term> <note>original: "Grad"; in Freemasonry, the second degree is that of "Fellow Craft."</note> after the end of the first year. This took place in July 1780. — A few new symbols! some different ceremonies! and an explanation that shared at least one thing with the first: ambiguity! — For a man whose expectations no longer outran his power of judgment, this was too little. A chance circumstance, however, contributed to keeping him—if not satisfied—at least in the good-natured mood that guards against impatience.

This man expressed his satisfaction with the correct explanation of the <term>hieroglyphs</term> <note>original: "Hieroglyphen"; in the 18th century, Freemasons often used this term to describe their symbolic diagrams and emblems, which they believed encoded ancient secrets.</note> (as they are called in the <term>Masonic language</term>), which he had just heard. — After the ceremonies ended, Cedrinus spoke with a closer friend, Brother N..., about the matter; he did not hide his unfulfilled expectations and remarked that only the involvement of men of insight and judgment, whom he knew within the <term>Order</term>, could persuade him to pursue these dark paths any further. Otherwise, he did not know what to say about the whole affair. “No information at all is given. One obscurity is merely exchanged for another; and I still know no more of the purpose or essence than I did before. The language heard here absolutely requires a translation; and yet no instruction is provided for it. Must I perhaps still wait for this?”

Brother N... did not actually provide what one would call a direct and clear answer. Rather, in a sequence prompted by the moment, he strung together a multitude of friendly words, by which he highly approved of and praised Cedrinus’s judgment and insight, agreed with him completely, and assured him with no small confidence that all his expectations would be fully satisfied.

## Second Chapter.

->Internal Unrest. — Investigations into purposes that come to an end without having ever begun.<-

<meta>This page continues the speaker's critique of the psychological atmosphere within secret societies. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, completing the thought that it is "dangerous and unwise" to prioritize ritualistic gravity over honest self-reflection.</meta>

unwise, when **secret societies** — which all bring into the world a certain natural tendency to experience the state just mentioned <note>Referring to the previous page’s discussion on the embarrassment of discovering that a group’s "secrets" are actually common human failings.</note> sooner or later — make the most appropriate and beneficial relief of that state more difficult for themselves by granting too much power to **solemnity** <note>original: "Feyerlichkeit"; in this context, it refers to the rigid, ritualistic gravity and overblown ceremony common in 18th-century fraternal organizations.</note>.

Among all the forms of **idolatry** with which humans have burdened themselves, the service of solemnity is the absolute worst, the most oppressive, and the most dangerous. What is to be expected from a deity that demands to be worshipped with the most anxious and submissive ceremonies, yet actually exists nowhere except in the facial features, the prescribed gestures, and the measured postures of its worshippers? These worshippers, at their core, can only remain devout as long as it pleases their eyes, noses, lips, hands, and feet. — A single smile robs the idol of its entire existence.

A society can therefore never submit to the **despotism of solemnity** without shackling investigation — or even excluding it entirely for the sole reason that there is a fear it might too easily disturb the expressions, gestures, and postures prescribed and essentially necessary for maintaining this iron constitution. What a precarious, one-sided, and limited **truth** must words contain when, in assembling them, the **speaker** is forced above all else to consider the faces and expressions of his listeners? How must the thought bend and distort itself if it is to be judged not so much by the intellect, but by the hearing of those present? For the sake of **solemnity**, the best orator is the one who assembles the most resonant words while possessing the fewest thoughts.

<meta>This page continues the author’s psychological analysis of the founders of the "Strict Observance" Masonic system. It explores how a fixation on historical ideals can create "optical illusions" of virtue, and moves into a broader historical reflection on the era of the Crusades and Chivalric Orders.</meta>

...showed [themselves], and which history has in part handed down in sketches according to the laws and the limits of its own art. — In staring fixedly at any one thing, it is only too easy for certain peculiar forms to take shape between the object and the eye—forms which have no existence other than that which they receive from our own gaze. <note>The author suggests that the "Strict Observance" founders were essentially hallucinating a glorious past that never existed, much like an optical illusion caused by staring too long at a single point.</note> And whoever possesses enough of an **inquiring spirit** <term>Untersuchungsgeist: a critical, analytical mind typical of Enlightenment thinking</term> to search more for that which forms the figures themselves than to grasp their proportions as they appear to him; whoever finds more pleasure in tracing the **inner unity** which manifests itself so differently in the virtues and great deeds of men—simply because each is granted specific circumstances under which he acts according to his ability—that person will hardly ever found an Order.

The period of the **Chivalric Orders** <term>Ritterorden: medieval military-religious orders like the Knights Templar or Teutonic Knights</term> seems to crown the actual heroic age of our and related nations by bringing it to a close. Through them, that raw strength—which we observe not without a secret pleasure because we view it from a distant vantage point—appears to us united into a mighty grandeur. The proportions and the true form of the individual members are hidden from us by this distance. We see more *that* everything is united, than *how* this union takes place. One might [compare] the closer [unions], precisely [formed] for special purposes...

<summary>The author reflects on how the "heroic age" of Chivalric Orders appears grand only from a distance, arguing that those with a truly critical mind see the human reality behind the myths and thus feel no need to revive such orders.</summary>
<keywords>inquiring spirit, inner unity, Chivalric Orders, heroic age, historical distance, optical illusion, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of historical chivalric orders, transitioning from the "optical illusion" of their perceived glory to a comparison with modern military structures. He argues that the grandeur of such "masses" comes at the cost of individual humanity.</meta>

...compare the combined **Chivalric Orders** <term>Ritterorden: military-religious orders of the Middle Ages, such as the Knights Templar</term> of those times with the sight of our **standing armies** <note>In the 18th century, the rise of professional "standing armies" was a major shift in European society, often criticized for their rigid, mechanical discipline.</note>. A multitude of people, whom we see moving with the full weight of a single great mass through art and order, fill our **imagination** <note>original: "Phantasie"</note> with images of superior strength and irresistible power. And we enjoy these uplifting sensations with deep satisfaction until we mentally dissolve the whole into its parts, and then see how every individual must suffer so that the whole can be active; how he must constrain himself so that the whole may expand; how he must sacrifice his own **personality** <note>original: "Persönlichkeit"</note> and suppress his powers against his will, in order—as a mere part among others of his kind who have also become nothing but parts—to produce the effect that inspires great feelings in others but causes him pain. Then the greatness and the uplifting nature of the sensation vanishes.

Indeed, the founders of the **Order of the Templars** <note>The Knights Templar, whose legacy was being claimed by various Masonic groups at the time this was written.</note> should have considered that a secret chivalric order would be both useless and wrong. Useless, because all the purposes it could fulfill were achievable in other ways; and wrong, because its foundation was not excused by its utility. They should have considered that every age [imposes] enough constraint on its people in their own **civil institution** <term>bürgerliche Einrichtung: the laws, social structures, and bureaucracies of organized society</term>...

No order is possible without **subordination**, which holds sway through its mere power. Only through this arrangement are spiritual and worldly orders maintained. And they have always had to grant their members an appropriate compensation for the freedom that was taken away.

Spiritual orders alone seem to find a treasure in the human **imagination** <note>original: "Phantasie"</note> with which they can pay for even the most unnatural sacrifices. 33

...content with the greatest **self-denial**, because everyone around us is as well. — But a **secret society**, which seeks to bring the already restricted human being into new restrictions, regulations, and connections, must entice, provoke, and seduce him, so that he pulls the yoke over his own neck in thoughtless goodwill. It must stir his expectations from a dark distance, pique his curiosity, and maintain his hope through a flattering state of activity.

This is what the founders of the **Strict Observance** <note>original: "stricten Observanz"; a high-degree Masonic rite founded in the mid-18th century by Baron von Hund, which claimed descent from the Knights Templar and demanded absolute obedience to "unknown superiors."</note> did; not because they wanted to, but because they had to. And because the founder of an **order** is its **superior** <term>Obere: the high-ranking leader or head of a religious or fraternal organization</term>: it is naturally easier for him to adapt to his own arrangement than it is for the subordinates. Also, he who stands at the head can exercise very strict **subordination** without even feeling the pleasure of ruling to an exceptional degree. He can take pleasure in his own activity without noticing how much he restricts other people. These others, however, feel what he hardly thinks of. They seek **compensation** <term>Ersatz: a reward or psychological satisfaction that makes a sacrifice feel worthwhile</term> while he barely notices that they are sacrificing anything. Of them, each one feels and thinks for his own person; he, the superior, thinks only of his "whole," which he strives to preserve, and views them as parts toward which he means very well, but which individually no longer...

The old **English Freemasonry** was a society of free people governed by **order**, not power; an order that was barely sufficient to maintain the society and was often too weak to withstand encroaching disorder. The system of **Strict Observance** <note>original: "stricten Observanz"; a Masonic system emphasizing hierarchical obedience and a supposed lineage from the Knights Templar.</note> had already spread throughout a large part of Germany—in **Upper Saxony**, **Franconia**, and along the **Rhine**—before the **Hamburg lodges** took it upon themselves. But even they could not always resist the lure of curiosity and the praises which always grow in proportion to their distance. The **Reformation** <note>original: "Reformation"; here referring to the formal "correction" or restructuring of the Masonic rite to align with the Strict Observance system.</note> soon took place; and it had its advantages, which the novelty placed in an even more brilliant light. The true nature of the matter became known to only a few, and then only slowly and imperfectly. Expectations thus had freer rein, never lacking for material to keep them occupied; and since man is generally a patient creature as long as he is not left alone, people remained occupied, entertained, and bored <note>original: "ennüyirt"; a Germanized version of the French *ennuyer*, reflecting the aristocratic language of the 18th century.</note> in this foreign fashion for over ten years without growing weary of it, or even closely examining for what purpose, according to what rules, and for whose benefit they were performing these exercises, of which the end seemed to be the best part,

The communication of this knowledge occurred through admission into higher and higher **degrees**; any other way would have been contrary to the **order**, and the order had been established. At the very top stood **princes** from illustrious houses, followed by men of reputation and irreproachable character; this made it even easier for every waiting member to forget his own insignificance in view of the importance of the whole.

But in **inner Germany** <note>likely referring to the more conservative or provincial territories of central Germany, as opposed to the cosmopolitan port city of Hamburg mentioned previously.</note>, in places where the inhabitants were less busy and took part in this **association** with greater seriousness, tranquility could not be maintained for so long. People disagreed, quarreled, and wrote against one another. Anyone reading these writings finds it difficult to grasp how so many secrets could suddenly make themselves heard in the world at once, without a single one of them appearing any less secret, less ancient, or less "the best" to its proponent, or any less new, less untrue, or less poor to his opponent. The superiors of the various branches of the **Order** had already held several meetings to establish a firmer whole and to overthrow the systems of their opponents. Finally, in the year 1780, an even more general assembly was called, but through various circumstances it was more delayed than expedited. It did not actually come to pass until 1782, and was held at **Wilhelmsbad** near **Hanau** <note>The Convent of Wilhelmsbad was a major turning point in Masonic history, where delegates from across Europe met to reform the "Strict Observance" rite and settle disputes over the Order's origins.</note>.

One can well imagine that no professional historians gathered for this purpose. The **Order of the Temple** <note>original: "Tempel-Orden"; here referring to the "Strict Observance" branch of Freemasonry which claimed descent from the medieval Knights Templar.</note> had possessed many specific inconveniences for its members in **Hamburg**. Its organization was fashioned too closely after the principles of those states in which an ancient prejudice, ingrained since time immemorial, separates and distinguishes those called <term>nobles<note>original: "Adliche"; refers to the hereditary aristocracy.</note></term> from those who can only call themselves <term>citizens<note>original: "Bürger"; in the context of a "Free Imperial City" like Hamburg, this refers to the mercantile middle class who held the actual power.</note></term>, treating them as if they were almost a different species of being. In places where this prejudice has become natural through long habit, it does not easily occur to one class of people to view the other as their equals. There, it is natural for the citizen to recognize the noble as a person of high rank, and for the noble to view the citizen as someone of low standing.

In **Hamburg**, by contrast, where almost the opposite prejudice prevails—where wealth confers prestige, and wealth is acquired through industriousness in trade—and where one looks upon the nobleman (as a person whose ability to earn is so restricted, yet who is considered a man of rank elsewhere) not without a certain pity for his constrained circumstances, an Order must have seemed all the more foreign and strange when it demanded from its knights the privilege of a nob- <note>The text cuts off here with the catchword "adli-", likely completing the thought as "noble birth" or "noble origin" on the following page.</note>

...secrets into their indispensable purpose and a condition of their existence, it has naturally become a silent law among their members to discuss the nature of their association in no other language than the only one suited for it. This is a language that needs no dictionary; for its words—if they were to receive a fixed, definite meaning—would lose all usefulness for the propagation and preservation of these secrets. In such conversations, one assumes, as if out of politeness, that one is understood. And this politeness is all the more necessary because one cannot abandon it without risking the peaceful and friendly character of the entire conversation.

During this period of his expectations, Cedrinus had often spoken with one or another of his Masonic <note>original: "maurerischen"; referring to Freemasonry, which in the 18th century was a central hub for social networking, philosophical debate, and the study of "secret" knowledge.</note> friends about the stories that were then occupying the public mind. Brother N... <note>The use of an initial followed by an ellipsis was a common 18th-century convention to suggest the person was a real individual whose identity was being protected, particularly within secret societies.</note>, a more intimate friend with whom he conversed more frequently, spoke so obscurely, agreed so readily with his opinions, and yet always let the matter be decided by his "higher wisdom," which always had at its service some riddle or oracular pronouncement that was in no way inferior in obscurity to those of antiquity. ...ghtly unclear. Nor were they very clear. On the other hand, the people personally present—his old acquaintances and friends—spoke in that high tone of certainty which borders so closely on the sublime <note>The "Sublime" (das Erhabene) was a central concept in 18th-century philosophy and aesthetics, referring to an greatness or intensity—often in nature or speech—that overwhelms the viewer's reason or emotions.</note> that it offers a connoisseur almost no other occupation than to silently translate what he hears into the common language of men, for his own benefit. 

**Cedrinus** did not hit upon this kind of translation, because he recognized the words being recited to him as expressions of his mother tongue; his experience had not yet provided him with enough data to make him familiar with that rhetorical figure—missing from all textbooks yet so effective—through whose use the speaker actually only *seems* to say something. Nor had he yet been led to study the nature of that deception which acts upon the human ear in much the same way as the magic lantern <term>Magic Lantern: An early type of image projector using transparent slides and a light source, often used in the 18th century to create illusions, phantasmagoria, or "magical" effects.</term> acts upon the eye. 

Much less had he reflected on the important phenomenon whereby mere empty words, for the one who utters them—as long as they occupy his tongue—possess a certain extremely transitory, fleeting appearance of truth. This appearance protects the speaker from the reproach of lying just as much as it elevates him out of the class of people who—know what they are saying.

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 43. Cedrinus is navigating the confusing, high-flown rhetoric of his associates, which he interprets through his own limited experience, unaware that their speech is designed to sound profound without actually conveying information. This page explores how he processed this "empty" communication and transitions into the historical mystery of the Knights Templar.</meta>

Over the long passage of time, he had become so accustomed to finding nothing but "food for thought" in everything he heard that he eventually came to expect nothing more than that—mere raw material for his mind. And indeed, what in this world is so insignificant that it cannot somehow provide a subject for reflection?

He received plenty of this "food for thought," because almost everything he heard consisted of statements that neither affirmed nor denied anything. Such sentences offer almost endless material for those willing to devote the time and effort to unravel them. There is no more deceptive way to steer someone’s thinking than by tossing out phrases that simultaneously provoke thought and spread confusion.

Even today, the true history of the Knights Templar <term>Knights Templar: A medieval Catholic military order. In the context of 18th-century Freemasonry, many groups claimed secret descent from the Templars, though historical evidence was often lacking.</term> remains many degrees removed from historical certainty, despite all the efforts of critical scholarship <note>original: "Kritik". In this context, it refers to the Enlightenment practice of "historical criticism," which seeks to separate myth from fact through rigorous analysis of sources.</note>. Cedrinus lacked the erudition to know the primary sources from which the meager facts of their history are drawn; nor did he possess the critical faculty to use those sources properly, even if he had known them. Among scholars, the Knights Templar had long been viewed with the intense interest that naturally surrounds those perceived as "innocent sufferers." <note>This likely refers to the tragic dissolution of the Order in 1312 and the execution of its leaders, which led many later writers to view them as victims of a political conspiracy.</note>

The larger number of brothers were led and allowed themselves to be led. All those who belonged to this group promoted a purpose unknown to them through a good will that was credited to them as a merit. The smaller, by far smaller number, ruled. These few were people who understood and loved ceremonies, could speak at length where clear concepts were not required, and where explanations were necessary, found a natural remedy in the silence that their needs taught them, and through which they served their cause without needing to exert their intellect.

Naturally, the expressions were always most significant and mysterious coming from those whose actual insights were the least. — Those who were less susceptible to this brand of piety withdrew without making a sound. — In general, no one was dissatisfied; yet only the few **Superiors**, <note>The "Superiors" (Obern) refers to the high-ranking officials within the Masonic hierarchy who directed the "inner" or secret work of the lodge.</note> who succeeded in their quiet activity, were likely truly satisfied. —

## Chapter Four.
### Various Occasions for One Outcome.

It is an observation that philosophers have often made, that sensations and even passions can be excited within us by objects which are not actually the cause of them, but which once appeared to us in very close association with the true cause through a chance connection. We need only re-enter the place and behold the same objects among which we once mourned the death of a dear friend, or indulged in grief over his departure: and we feel ourselves involuntarily plunged into a tender melancholy, for which not a single string of our heart was tuned just a few minutes before. The activity of our imagination is determined without our even knowing it; and we experience in our innermost feelings a long and interesting history, for which the connection of chronological sequence provides not the slightest occasion. — This is what so often makes human actions inexplicable. This is what leads the most skillful syllogist <term>syllogist: A practitioner of formal logic who uses deductive reasoning—specifically syllogisms—to reach a conclusion.</term> into labyrinths from which he will not be able to unwind himself even with Ariadne's thread, <note>original: "Knäuel der Ariadne". In Greek mythology, Ariadne provided Theseus with a ball of thread to find his way out of the Minotaur's Labyrinth; it is a common metaphor for a logical path through a complex problem.</note> if he has forgotten the signifying

<summary>The author discusses how human emotions and actions are often triggered by subconscious associations with objects or places rather than direct logic. This psychological complexity, he argues, can lead even the most rigorous logical thinkers into a "labyrinth" of inexplicable behavior.</summary>

<keywords>psychology of association, memory, philosophy of emotion, logic, syllogism, Ariadne's thread, human behavior</keywords>

Like magic, an entire world forms within us, whose time, place, and life exist only in us and in our enchanting imagination. <term>imagination: The German *Einbildungskraft* refers to the active power of the mind to "build" internal images or representations.</term> We seldom distinguish the internal from the external; and therefore, man usually recognizes the error only upon his distancing himself from it.

The devotion of one man is comical <note>original: "possierlich" — also meaning droll, drollery, or ridiculous in a lighthearted way.</note> to another. — What an immense diversity! And yet human nature is one and the same.

But perhaps it is not as settled as is commonly accepted that — our worship is determined by the object. Perhaps in this whole secret process of our nature, there is more of the passionate than we can easily perceive. — We love beautiful forms; and yet it is not the beautiful body which excites the passion of love within us. It is so difficult for us to explain love; perhaps it is due to the similarity that devotion has with it that we experience the same thing. Only very few people strive for an explanation of love; and yet all love more or less in their lives. How few philosophers have sought to explain devotion! Until we possess this explanation, and until we are better able to determine and distinguish how much of the sad phenomena of religious perversion and superstition <term>superstition: *Aberglauben*—literally "over-belief" or "mis-belief," often used in the 18th century to describe irrational religious practices.</term> among humanity may perhaps be attributed to a disproportionate dominance of a faculty <term>faculty: *Vermögen* refers to an inherent power or capacity of the mind, such as reason, imagination, or will.</term> that we all share in common, it will likely always remain too difficult for us to form a clear conception of the possibility of those ancient mysteries of paganism. Never, in all of recorded history, has a religion been practiced by a people that stands in such intimate connection with reason and knowledge as that of the Protestant Christians. ...not led by mere curiosity to those mysteries.

Theology, which has always held so much importance in our fatherland, had already become increasingly popular at that time. It had fully retained its scientific <term>scientific: *wissenschaftlich*—in this context, the term refers to rigorous, systematic academic scholarship rather than just the natural sciences.</term> prestige; but it now seemed to want to make everyone a participant in that science. And who would not gladly become a participant in such a science? Religion is omnipotent as soon as it employs the ordering hand of science. Everyone then seeks the ultimate ground of their imperfect knowledge; and everyone finds satisfaction. To morality and natural law <term>natural law: *Recht der Natur*—the philosophical concept of a universal system of right or justice held to be common to all humans and derived from nature or God.</term>, theology provides a fundamental law in the will of God, which man reveres as soon as he recognizes it; it breathes life into physics, and it gives a soul to metaphysics; only for logic alone—which one might call the immutable—does theology have no living breath. This remains always a dead instrument in its hand. A dead instrument; but a very stubborn one. The learned theologian finds in logic both the scale and the guiding rule of his thoughts; and a learned opponent constantly refers him back to it. For the unlearned, the theologian becomes "popular," deviates from logic, and follows the more powerful asceticism <term>asceticism: *Ascetik*—here referring to the branch of theology dealing with spiritual exercises and the practical cultivation of piety.</term>.

Yet science only *seems* to become popular; it never truly does. It is art that speaks to feelings and sensations, and often [be-] <note>original: "be-" — This is a syllable of a word that continues on the next page (likely *begeistert*, *bewirkt*, or *belohnt*).</note>

...comfortable words borrowed from the former. — — Art always finds numerous pupils when she becomes a teacher; and she is an excellent teacher. Rigorous science <term>science: *Wissenschaft*—in this 18th-century context, the term implies systematic academic study and scholarly rigor rather than just the modern natural sciences.</term> demands that we should never believe her; she is far too fond of proving; art tells us our own thoughts in her own words. We never tire of listening to her; for she speaks beautifully. We could gladly dispense with all science, if only without it art could keep its spiritual nature immaculate and pure enough, and did not too easily become coarse and earthly.

What a dead, charmless thing is Newton's entire system compared to a physico-theology <term>physico-theology: A popular 18th-century intellectual movement that sought to prove the existence and attributes of God through the observation of nature and the physical world.</term> that combines its art with his science! But what a lowly bungling, entirely unworthy of any art, does physico-theology become if it does not know his scientific system or wishes to disdain it! In the structure of the world, which exists through attractive force <note>original: *anziehende Kraft*—referring to Newtonian gravity.</note>, the author of all life and the ruler of spirits is missing—the Supreme Being, in whose infinite perfection we recognize that which is noblest in ourselves as incomparably more noble. Physico-theology presents God to us as the creator and sustainer of all that is; as the ruler not only of the spirits, but also of the physical world.

If it did not also depend upon the inner eye of our soul how the spiritual image appears to us, then we would need nothing further. But let us only neglect or disdain to train the eye of the spirit on spiritual things: it will become so nearsighted and dull <note>original: *blöde*—in the 18th century, this term described a weakness of the senses or a lack of mental clarity rather than a lack of intelligence.</note> that the truly spiritual is no longer visible to it at all, and conversely, the coarsest matter appears as spiritual. Let us only neglect that rigorous and uncharming science <term>science: *Wissenschaft*—here meaning systematic, disciplined scholarly study.</term> which laboriously and slowly grants us few, but ever more and ever more accurate, insights into nature: and we shall at last lose all capacity to recognize the perfect so completely that the most imperfect of all is perfect enough for us to satisfy our diminished need for a Supreme Being. 

— Thus did peoples once come to recognize their deities in lowly animals! And thus there is also still in our enlightened century a path that leads through the goodness of God in nature to the philosopher’s stone <note>original: *Stein der Weisen*—the legendary substance of alchemy believed to transmute base metals into gold and provide the elixir of life.</note>.

Cedrinus <note>Likely a pseudonym or a character used to illustrate a philosophical point, typical of Enlightenment-era dialogues or essays.</note> entered upon this path unawares; and only his good, sound common sense advised him to turn back when, after a few steps, he glimpsed from afar the labyrinths in which errors multiply to infinity through their own connections. — Theology addresses common human understanding in the most effective way by acting as physico- <note>The text breaks here; it likely concludes as "physico-theology" on the following page.</note>

There is in devotion an important, decisive, but highly delicate point where only good taste can keep it pure. *Cedrinus* had not spent long enough in the school of the arts. He had not exercised and strengthened the powers of his youth on the masterpieces of the poetry and eloquence of antiquity. He had not been led by this path into the true greatness and beauty of the ancient world, and he had also not become more closely acquainted with the true form of the raw barbarism of those peoples, which distance usually represents so deceptively to us. Therefore, it was too easily possible for him to mistake an untrue image here and there for a real object, and to fail to notice that the best of what he found in the matter had been added from his own mind.

## Chapter Five

->One expectation leads to another.<-

It was the first Scottish Lodge <note>original: *Schottische Loge*. These "higher" degrees in Freemasonry often claimed to possess deeper philosophical or mystical secrets than the basic three degrees of the "Blue Lodge."</note> held since the Templar system <note>The "Strict Observance," which claimed Masons were heirs to the Knights Templar, had recently collapsed after the Convent of Wilhelmsbad in 1782.</note> had been abandoned, in July 1783. The explanation was obscure and provided few clues. These, along with the figures, pointed toward things other than what Cedrinus had expected.

A few days later, he was in company with Brother N—. The latter beckoned to him, went with him into another room, and asked: "How did you like the initiation?"

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how different readers approach a text—specifically the personification of Wisdom. ...how a poet of a foreign land and a long-past age expresses thoughts that he believes he can still understand in his own country and in his own time; all the more will he strive to puzzle out what is obscure to him within it. He does not merely observe the poem, but rather it acts upon him. He does not admire the enthusiasm, but is instead carried away by it; and even the weakest echo of the voice from heaven remains a source of instruction. Personified Wisdom, who for the critic <term>Kunstrichter: literally an "art-judge," referring to a professional literary or aesthetic critic</term> exists only within the poem, becomes for him a historical being; the life that the poet has granted her becomes a testimony to her reality; and every reference wins more of his confidence the more accurately it states the characteristics which she—took from that very description.

Bad people are led astray by stupidity; the better among us can be misled by their love of the beautiful. Yet it is not so much this heavenly love that misleads them. It is their own lack of attention. They wish to contemplate the beautiful, glorious figure closer and closer still, and in doing so forget to cast a glance at the bad paths through which a guide—whom they only hear, but do not see—leads them by the hand.

C. seemed to hint at a more intimate acquaintance with that very wisdom to the inquiring man—who hoped for more instruction than he wished to investigate through mere principles and comparisons. This wisdom is praised in a chapter of a biblical book attributed to Solomon <note>Likely referring to the *Wisdom of Solomon*, a book in the Apocrypha that was highly valued by mystical and alchemical traditions.</note>, and which he was now studying upon the recommendation of an initiate <term>Eingeweiheten: an initiate; one who has been "led into" secret knowledge or mystery rites</term> in order to prepare himself for further revelations.

Whatever one does, mediocrity helps, at most, only with preservation and safety. To gain anything, one must dare the extreme and employ all one's strength. If everything is lost despite great effort, then nothing would have been gained with less effort anyway. This rule also applies to the liar and the boaster. It is a wretched thing to boast a little and lie a little. The listener or reader immediately has the yardstick ready, because the speaker remains within the listener's own circle of ideas. On the other hand, even the most extreme exaggeration makes a matter more believable, provided the author possesses enough skill to represent what is untrue as something unfathomable. We love

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Rosicrucian "Assembly Speech." ...[we] all love the marvelous <note>original: *das Wunderbare*; in the 18th-century context, this refers to things that transcend the natural order or everyday logic, often found in myths and fairy tales.</note>; and in order to revive this love within us, it requires only a kind of elucidation that takes the place of an explanation, in that it tells us even more about the wonder—a wonder that the explanation would destroy if it made it comprehensible. In that Assembly Speech <note>referring to the Rosicrucian "Assembly Speech of the R. C. of the Old System" mentioned on the previous page.</note> there are quite shameless and ridiculous boastings. But it is precisely through this that they become effective, because they approach the historical. — Shaftesbury <note>Anthony Ashley-Cooper, 3rd Earl of Shaftesbury (1671–1713), an English philosopher who famously argued in his *Characteristics of Men, Manners, Opinions, Times* that "ridicule is the test of truth."</note> was mistaken in his expression when he designated the ridiculous as the touchstone of truth in general. Only poetic truth can be tested by the ridiculous. Historical truth can be tested by it as little as philosophical truth. The most ridiculous thing imaginable has been experienced by a hundred people; and it does not become untrue simply because it is recounted. — The Assembly Speech of the R. C. <note>Rosicrucians</note> is very ridiculous if one considers it as a speech, as a work of art. But insofar as it boldly tells people mendacious tales to their faces, it lies only for those who judge its tales according to general principles of plausibility. Now, however, in human life, everyone basically has their own particular rules of what is plausible; and only the philosopher sifts through his rules until he attains principles. The rules

<meta>This page continues the discussion on why certain texts, despite their "ridiculous" or "shameless" claims, gain credibility. It specifically focuses on how pre-existing knowledge and "historical" framing influence the protagonist, Cedrinus, as he engages with Rosicrucian writings.</meta>

...of the plausible is dictated to everyone by their own experience, and when we appeal to it in a case of decision-making, it is usually only the foreign or the familiar in the objects that we actually want to determine.

The existence of a secret, high wisdom was now something familiar to Cedrinus <note>Cedrinus is likely a pseudonym or a literary persona used by the author to represent an initiate or a seeker of secret knowledge.</note>. Bit by bit, he had read, heard, and found so many and such varied hints and proofs about it—and imagined he had found his way here or there—that the historical aspect of this mystery was no longer new to him. Whatever he heard further about it gained all the more plausibility <note>original: *Glaublichkeit*; referring to how believable or credible a claim seems based on prior context.</note> the better it agreed with the old information and seemed to say just a little bit more. Had the "Assembly Speech of the R. C." <note>R. C. stands for *Rosae Crucis* or Rosicrucians, a legendary and secretive intellectual movement of the 17th and 18th centuries claiming secret wisdom.</note> been the first thing he had seen concerning the secret wisdom, it would certainly have made a completely different impression on his still-unbiased mind. 

But as it was, it only gave further news of a matter whose existence had already been more "betrayed," as it were, than proven to him through so many different paths for quite some time. Its author spoke of it with the confidence and firmness of a witness. He had, as Cedrinus (along with every other reader of the speech) had to assume, spoken all this in an assembly that could at least judge the historical truth of his assertions.

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of a Rosicrucian speech. The narrator critiques the rhetorical strategies used by the speaker to manipulate the audience's emotions rather than providing logical proof for his claims about the "Natural Work of Art."</meta>

Certainly, not the slightest proof is given for this bold assertion. But instead, the speaker does something that serves his purpose better. He has aroused astonishment. He takes advantage of this situation and makes a transition to worship. “God! How kind you are!” he cries out. He leaves the half-believing reader—whom he has now led into a region that is not entirely unfamiliar—not a single quiet moment to look around searchingly and thoughtfully. The imagination is excited. The heart must complete the work, and emotion <note>original: *Rührung*; a state of being deeply moved or sentimental, a key concept in 18th-century psychology where feeling was often prioritized over cold logic.</note> must take the place of conviction. — “Here fall down, O human race! be amazed, tremble and worship!” — “Yes! You were still loving, Merciful One!” etc.

A critic <note>original: *Kunstrichter*; literally an "art-judge," a common Enlightenment-era term for a literary reviewer or cultural critic who evaluates the merits of a work.</note> who is accustomed to such things will certainly not be carried away by this enthusiastic transition. For a critic, the author of an “Assembly Speech of the R. C.” <note>R. C. stands for the *Rosae Crucis* or Rosicrucians, the secret mystical society being described.</note> offers nothing more than—in any case—an impertinence. But the critic, if he overlooks nothing, can well judge what impression such a lyrical-rhetorical flourish makes on people whose imagination is no longer unbiased, and whose heart is touched here by impressions that recall a hundred similar ones from the earliest times of their youth, without them even noticing it.

The period in which, with all the power of eloquence <note>original: *Beredtsamkeit*; here referring to the persuasive oratory used in religious instruction or mystical initiation.</note> (which need not be great to affect us at that age) and with all the gravity that is due to what is holy, a compressed summary of both the historical and the dogmatic aspects of our religion is pressed upon our hearts rather than submitted for our critical examination, falls precisely into the years of fully awakening life. <meta>This page continues the critique of the mystical speech given to Cedrinus. It contrasts the academic learning of a scholar with the experience-based reasoning of the common person, ultimately defining the process of "Enlightenment" as the application of common sense to historical untruths.</meta>

...which starts from principles and seeks principles, would be repugnant to him as something premature.

The scholar learns principles in order to recite them again; but the principles which a person gains outside of this professional class come *after* experiences, because experiences provide **reason** <term>Vernunft; the faculty of logical thought and understanding</term> with the material from which it forms its propositions. And it is basically such propositions by which the scholar, in time—once he has cast off his prejudices—begins to test his learned principles. **Scholarship** <term>Gelehrsamkeit; formal, academic learning or erudition</term> can make it so that someone, for example, finds the word **tinctural** <term>tincturalisch; an alchemical term referring to a purifying spiritual essence</term> offensive when used in connection with the **blood of reconciliation** <note>original: "Versöhnungsblute"; referring to the theological concept of Christ's atonement</note> as the speech uses it; but scholarship alone cannot teach him to recognize as false the entire incredible story of supernaturally communicated science (as it continues in the speech), the essence of which is kept secret even as its history is shared. Only **philosophy** can teach him this, by demonstrating what is impossible. This is a long lesson; and it must also be tedious and difficult because we still have so many untruths in history generally, which the scholar believes, and which **common sense** <term>gemeine Menschenverstand; literally "common human understanding"</term> would have to reject if only it were applied there. Bit by bit, it is applied to each of these individual untruths; and that is what we call **Enlightenment** <term>Aufklärung; the intellectual movement emphasizing reason and evidence over dogma and tradition</term>.

->**Vocabulary**<-
Principles, Scholarship, Reason, Philosophy, Enlightenment, Common Sense, Tinctural

<meta>This page continues the critique of how scholarship and experience interact to reveal historical or mystical untruths. It specifically explores the psychology of "Cedrinus"—likely a pseudonym for a candidate in a secret society—explaining why he was initially susceptible to the "incredible" claims of the mystical speech mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

It happens, however, only after experience has led the way. **Scholarship** <term>Gelehrsamkeit; formal, academic learning</term> aids in this process only to the extent that scholars are also **philosophers** <term>Philosophen; here used in the sense of critical thinkers who seek underlying truths</term>. At the time when the untruths, which were linked in that speech with widely accepted news, were still new, even the scholars did not recognize them as unbelievable.

The truth depends solely on **reason** <term>Vernunft; the faculty of logical thought</term>; but what is believable depends on our individual capacity to apply that reason. To Cedrinus, what he was yet to experience could not be perceived as unbelievable. And precisely because this experience was still in the future, his **fantasy** <term>Phantasie; the mental faculty of forming images and ideas</term> could shape its nature to align with what he most preferred to expect. 

He thought neither of **ambition** <term>Ehrgeiz</term>, a **thirst for power** <term>Regiersucht; the drive to rule or dominate others</term>, nor **self-interest** <term>Eigennutz</term>. Therefore, he never even arrived at the point of considering the **association** <note>The "Verbindung" or secret society that Cedrinus is seeking to join</note> he intended to enter in light of these three factors—which are actually the three primary considerations in any analysis of an association of several people. 

Cedrinus surrendered himself to the expectations that his imagination beautified, and to the pious thoughts that his sentiments suggested to him—which his **creative imagination** <term>Einbildungskraft</term> brought to life—and occupied his expectations with a future of high insight. He could only estimate <note>The original German "zu schä-" is a catchword for the next page, completing the word "schätzen" (to estimate, value, or appreciate).</note> the value of this insight according to his own wishes, his humility, and his benevolence—

<summary>The author explains that "Cedrinus" was misled by his own imagination and lack of experience, failing to see the potential for ambition or self-interest in the secret society he wished to join. It argues that while truth is objective, believability is subjective and often colored by one's personal desires and lack of critical "philosophy."</summary>

## Chapter Seven.

### Initiation.

**O**ccupied with devout expectations, and not without a certain anxious vigilance over his own thoughts, Cedrinus went at the appointed time to the house of his Masonic and Scottish brother <term>Schottischer Bruder; a member of the "Scottish" degrees of Freemasonry, which were considered higher or more "secret" than the basic craft degrees</term>, N— <note>The use of an initial followed by a dash was a common convention in 18th-century writing to provide a sense of authenticity while protecting the identity of real-life figures</note>.

A serving Masonic brother <term>Dienender Bruder; a member of the fraternity who performed domestic or administrative duties within the Lodge</term>, whom he knew from the Lodge, was already waiting for him and led him into a private room.

He waited a little while; then Brother N— himself appeared, friendly, pleased, and obliging. Into the expression of the polite man receiving a welcome guest, there was mixed a small addition of solemnity; yet it was so faint, light, and natural that it only shimmered through because it was impossible to hide it completely. He was not the kind of man to put on a solemn face; rather, the importance of the occasion was too powerful for him. While he was happy to see his friend, the purpose of his visit was too vividly present in his mind, and everything that the expectation of this purpose held out in the distance as a reward...

He was the same man, but in a certain state of exaltation <note>A state of spiritual or emotional elevation common in 18th-century descriptions of religious or fraternal fervor</note>.

He said to Cedrinus in only a few short words: "now he would be initiated into higher secrets"—and shortly thereafter he asked him "whether he had Scottish attire <term>schottische Bekleidung; the specific regalia, often including a distinct apron and jewel, required for the Scottish Master degree</term> with him?"

A fellow Mason <note>original: "Freymaurerbruder"</note>, also known to him, opened the door for the Preparer and for him.

The door was closed once again.

The room was spacious, quiet, lit by candles, and all access to daylight was blocked. At the head of the room stood a rectangular table, draped in black. Upon it lay the Bible open, and beside it the laws, the register <term>Matrikel; the official roll or membership book of the lodge</term>, the instructions of the Senior Warden <term>Obervorsteher; one of the principal officers of a Masonic lodge, responsible for supervising the brothers</term>, and an apron, trimmed in black, along with an ornament.

Lodge, "which the philosophers explore to its center through diligence and labor, searching through its three kingdoms of nature <note>The three kingdoms refer to the animal, vegetable, and mineral realms, which alchemists studied to understand the secrets of creation</note>."

Cedrinus listened with close attention to the names assigned to the images he saw drawn here together. With these significant expressions—not one of which was lost on him—he could only barely connect the ideas that were most vividly and actively present in his memory; so quickly did one object follow another to complete the sequence.

The Presiding Officer <term>Obervorsteher; the high-ranking official leading the ceremony</term> made a short pause and concluded with the words: "Here, worthy Brother, you have the true and genuine discovery of the hieroglyphic images <note>In the 18th century, 'hieroglyphic' often referred to any symbolic or emblematic image used to hide spiritual or alchemical secrets</note> which you saw in part during your first reception into parabolic masonry <note>The Rosicrucians referred to standard Freemasonry as 'parabolic' or 'blue' masonry, viewing it as a mere introductory allegory to their own 'true' theoretical and practical work</note>. —— You will surely understand that the future occupation of the Theoretical Brothers <note>Theoretical Brother was the specific title for members of this degree in the Rosicrucian hierarchy</note> will be the investigation of the three kingdoms of nature. Consider well, worthy Brother, what great advantages you may draw from your investigations if you implore God for wisdom through earnest prayer. —— Through these, you will firstly come to know the Almighty Architect of the World better, and consequently glorify Him with more reverence; secondly, you will [perceive] His unfathomable wisdom and omnipotence in all [things...]

<summary>The Presiding Officer completes the explanation of the lodge's symbols, instructing Cedrinus that his primary duty as a "Theoretical Brother" is the spiritual and scientific study of the three kingdoms of nature to better understand God.</summary>

<keywords>initiation, Hieroglyphic images, Parabolic Masonry, Three Kingdoms of Nature, Architect of the World, Theoretical Brothers, alchemy, Cedrinus</keywords>

„...admire the **bodies of nature**—thirdly, you will arrive at the **knowledge of yourself**, and thereby contemplate the masterpiece of the Creator <note>In Rosicrucian philosophy, the "masterpiece" refers to the human being, viewed as the "Microcosm" or a miniature version of the entire universe</note> with astonishment. — Regarding all of this, dear Brother, you shall receive instruction bit by bit, as much as my weak powers allow, if only you wish to have patience and find pleasure in following me as a seeker and zealous pursuer of the truth.“

Following this promise—the conditions of which, patience and obedience, Cedrinus seemed to accept on his part through his silence—the **Presiding Officer** <term>Obervorsteher; the high-ranking official leading the ceremony</term> assigned him his place in the society. It was the second-to-last seat. „Here, my Brother,“ he said, „we take our seats according to our **seniority** <term>Anciennität; the chronological order in which members were initiated into the Order</term> in the **Freemasonic Order**. And according to this order, everyone also gives their voice in our **deliberations**. The elder brother speaks first, and then the one admitted immediately after him who is present among us. The distinctions and **ranks** <term>Chargen; specific administrative offices or titles like Warden or Orator</term> by which a brother admitted later often precedes an elder brother in those lower degrees <note>In "Blue" or standard Freemasonry, an officer of the lodge ranks higher than a regular member regardless of how long they have been a Mason.
