# Laws,
### for the highly exalted assembly of the theoretical brothers.

<note>The "Theoretical Brothers" (Theoricus) represent the second degree of the Gold and Rosy Cross. At this stage, members were expected to move beyond the moral symbols of Freemasonry and begin studying the actual secrets of nature and alchemy.</note>

->§. 1.<-

Since the true happiness of man consists in the pursuit of virtue and wisdom: the philosophical brothers have wished to exhort [one another], that after diligent and industrious care for their worldly affairs according to their status and office, they should also turn toward the **knowledge of nature**, which knowledge is an infallible means, through the grace of God, to arrive at true wisdom.

<note>In the 18th century, "knowledge of nature" (Naturerkenntniß) was seen as a religious act; by understanding the creation, the seeker believed they were getting closer to the mind of the Creator.</note>

->§. 2.<-

### Concerning Religion.

Every theoretical brother must be adherent to a certain religion, and follow it diligently and zealously, as is consistent with the duties of an honest man. Furthermore, everyone is free to give assent to those opinions which he considers the most rational and salutary; but only to those opinions that make a person pious, upright, fair, kind-hearted, and ready to serve his neighbor. Furthermore, it is enough if

<note>This reflects the "Enlightened" religious tolerance of the era. While members had to be practicing Christians (usually), the Order prioritized the *fruits* of faith—good character and neighborly love—over specific denominational dogmas.</note>

<summary>This page lists the fundamental statutes for the "Theoretical" degree of the Rosicrucians, outlining the pursuit of wisdom through nature and the requirement for members to be religious yet tolerant.</summary>
<keywords>Statutes, Theoricus degree, Rosicrucianism, virtue, wisdom, study of nature, religious tolerance, Enlightenment piety</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the fundamental statutes of the "Theoretical" degree (Theoricus), the second rank of the Order of the Gold and Rosy Cross. It completes the rule on religious requirements before moving into the social and political expectations of the members.</meta>

he is of the Christian lineage and faith, regardless of what land he may otherwise be from.

---

->§. 3.<-

### Concerning the Submission Owed to Authorities.

Every member of this society must be a loyal subject of his sovereign, a willing subordinate to his authority, a peaceful citizen and inhabitant of his place of residence, a mediator of disputes, and a promoter of peace and unity. For this reason, he must take the utmost care never to involve himself in any rebellions harmful to the state. And for this very reason in general, as well as for their consistently observed blameless conduct, this society has been supported at all times by kings, princes, and states.

<note>In the late 18th century, secret societies like the Rosicrucians were often under suspicion of harboring revolutionary or anti-monarchical ideas. This statute serves as a "loyalty oath," reassuring the ruling class that the Order’s pursuit of hidden knowledge would not disrupt the social order.</note>

---

->§. 4.<-

### Concerning the Admission of the Theoretical Brothers.

No others shall be admitted to this level except for <term>Scottish Masters</term> <note>original: "Schottische Meister." In the 18th-century masonic hierarchy, the "Scottish Master" degree was an advanced rank. The Rosicrucians used Freemasonry as a "recruitment pool," requiring members to have already achieved this rank before they could begin the "theoretical" (alchemical) studies of the Order.</note> who have shown sufficient proof of their <term>piety</term> <note>original: "Gottesfurcht," literally "fear of God."</note>, love for humanity, and desire for wisdom. When such a Scottish Master has been found, he shall, after the payment of his reception—

<note>The text ends mid-sentence here; "reception" likely refers to the "reception fees" or duties required for the initiation ceremony into the second degree.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the requirements for political loyalty to the state and specifies that only "Scottish Master" Freemasons are eligible for advancement into the Theoretical degree.</summary>
<keywords>loyalty to authorities, Scottish Master, requirements for admission, civic duty, Theoricus degree, Gold and Rosy Cross</keywords>

### §. 5.

### Concerning the Order which the Brethren Shall Maintain within the Assembly.

It is presupposed that the assembly consists entirely of worthy and inquisitive Brethren; consequently, the Worshipful Chief Superior <note>original: "Ehrwürdige Obervorsteher." This title refers to the presiding officer of the Rosicrucian circle or lodge.</note> expects brotherly love, peace, and unity, attentiveness, and a moral conduct that may serve as an example for the other Brethren to emulate.

All criticism, all subtle or pedantic questions, mockery, laughter, and idle chatter, as well as all discourteous actions in general, are therefore forbidden, as all such things are contrary to brotherly love and disturb good order.

The holding of the assembly shall be announced several days in advance to all Brethren by the Brother Secretary, and every Brother is requested to appear at the appointed time and hour, to bring his robe and his <term>jewels</term> <note>original: "Kleinodien." These are the symbolic insignia or medals worn by members to indicate their rank and office within the Order.</note>; without a <term>dispensation</term> <note>A formal exemption from the rules granted by a high-ranking officer.</note> from the Chief Superior, he may not enter without them.

<vocab>Brethren, Assembly, Chief Superior, Secretary, Jewels (Insignia), Order, Dispensation</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the admission requirements for the Theoretical degree and outlines the strict rules of decorum and punctuality required during meetings. It emphasizes the importance of brotherly love and the wearing of proper ritual regalia.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucian statutes, lodge conduct, Chief Superior, Masonic jewels, fraternal order, ritual regalia, 18th-century protocol</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the administrative statutes of the "Theoretical" degree of the Gold and Rosy Cross (Theoreticus) and records the final ritual steps for a new initiate, including their enrollment in the register.</meta>

If a brother arrives half an hour later than the appointed time, he must submit to paying a fine for the poor.

However, if a brother cannot appear at all for important reasons, he must have this reported to the Brother Secretary on that same morning. Such an absent brother is free to inquire publicly at the next assembly about what transpired in the previous meeting by consulting the minutes <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "Protocoll." The formal record of proceedings and deliberations of the lodge.</note> that are kept.

To which laws all philosophical Theorists <term>Theorists</term> <note>original: "philosophische Theoristen." This refers to members of the "Theoreticus" degree, the second grade in the nine-degree system of the Gold and Rosy Cross.</note> are bound to submit themselves most precisely.

May God grant blessing, peace, and unity to all worthy Brethren.

The Brother Master of Ceremonies answered with a composed expression and slowly: "When the work has reached perfection."

The Worshipful Chief Superior asked again: "What time is it at the end of the work?"

The Secretary answered: — Out of Chaos.

**Chief Superior** <note>original: "O. Vorst." abbreviation for "Obervorsteher."</note> What was Chaos?

**Secretary.** It was a dark, shapeless lump, which consisted of water and fire, and is indicated by the Hebrew word <note>original: "השמים" (Ha-shamayim). While typically translated as "the heavens," in this esoteric context it is interpreted as a compound of "Esh" (fire) and "Mayim" (water).</note> the heavens. Out of this lump, all things of this world were created through the almighty word of God. This was the Matter <note>original: "Materia."</note> in which all forms and shapes were contained in their potential, and were subsequently revealed through the will of the Creator.

**Chief Superior.** How did this come about?

**Secretary.** The Spirit of the Lord hovered upon the water, and through His life-giving power, He animated the Chaos, separated the light from the darkness, filled the void, illuminated the dark, set apart the things that were mixed together, ripened the raw with His divine warmth, made the barren fertile, and brought order to that which was disordered.

**Chief Superior.** What arose from the separation of Chaos?

**Secretary.** The angelic, the heavenly, and the elemental world.

**Chief Superior.** What is the angelic world?

**Secretary.** The seven hierarchies of angels, which were divided into legions, such as the Cherubim, Seraphim, Thrones, Dominions, Principalities, Powers, and Virtues <note>These ranks follow the traditional celestial hierarchy established by medieval theologians, representing different levels of proximity to the divine.</note>, which

**Chief Superior** <note>original: "O. Vorst."</note> What is the heavenly world?
**Secretary.** The seven planets and all the stars of the firmament.

**Chief Superior.** What is the sun?
**Secretary.** It is the purest and most perfect body, which was drawn out of the light, and is therefore capable of receiving <note>original: "concipiren"</note> the created light as it flows out from its center <note>original: "Centro"</note> through intermediate means <note>original: "per media"</note>, and communicating it to the other stars and things.

**Chief Superior.** What is the moon?
**Secretary.** The moon is a planet that received its body from the purest part of the abyss, and receives its light from the sun, giving that light a physical form <note>original: "corporisiret"</note> with its own essence, and then, little by little, communicating it in turn to the lower things.

**Chief Superior.** From where do the other planets receive their light?
**Secretary.** The remaining planets and stars all obtain their light from the sun, acting as the general receiver of the created light. On the other hand, the other stars constantly cast the essence of their own bodies back to the sun according to their order; thus, the sun is maintained in its power and radiance,

**Chief Superior.** Why the Spirit first?

**Secretary.** Because the Spirit, both in the Godhead and in the creatures, is the fundamental essence and primary matter <note>original: "Urstoff"</note> of all things.

**Chief Superior.** What did God ordain with this Spirit at the foundation of this world?

**Secretary.** The Almighty let this Spirit, proceeding from Him, seek the center-point of the world-structure, to seat and contract itself there; in which center-point it could give birth to more beings from itself, and then extend itself from the center-point of its dominion into a proper circumspherical <note>original: "circumsphärische," referring to a spherical extension outward from a center</note> distance.

**Secretary.** Certainly; for we see with our own eyes that without contraction, even now, no thing—be it animal, plant, or metal—can come into being; and consequently, this world-structure would never have come to light.

**Chief Superior.** What other kind of being did the Spirit create through the aforementioned contraction?

**Secretary.** A second kind of its essence, which we call the **Soul**.

**Secretary.** In the movement of contraction, the Spirit—through the compression of its own essence—detached a portion of its being from itself, thereby drawing it together more densely than its original essence had been. This, from the Spirit...

**9. Chief Superior.** <note>original: "9. Vorst." abbreviated for "Ober-Vorsteher," the head of a lodge or society.</note> Was the soul, with its contracting power, sufficient to represent the great structure of the world?

**9. Chief Superior.** What kind of being was this?

The soul is therefore only an altered essence of the Spirit, and the body is an altered and denser essence of the soul.

## Chapter One.

->Which books were recommended for Cedrinus to read.<-

---

Cedrinus now seemed, in a sense, to have entered a school. Everything led him to conclude that here, not only obedience but also practice was required; that it was not enough for instruction alone to benefit him, but that his own investigation into his purpose would be particularly fruitful <note>The author uses the word "ersprießlich," which conveys a sense of growth and productivity, much like a plant flourishing.</note>. — If only he had been clearly aware of this purpose! But it hovered obscurely before his eyes. His inclination was increasingly abandoned to his imagination <term>Einbildungskraft: in 18th and 19th-century philosophy, this refers to the faculty of forming mental images, which can lead one away from reality if not balanced</term>, and so what might have become <term>Wille</term> (will) and <term>Entschlossenheit</term> (determination) under the influence of judging reason was lost in mere wishes and longing. On this path, he distanced himself further and further from calm examination, which alone can serve as the basis for approval or disapproval.

Anyone who prefers to speak about morality rather than reflect on it can derive from it some aids toward a semblance of method as well as sophistry.

## Chapter Two.

### Testimony of a clergyman concerning the hidden sons of wisdom.

---

Far more important was the influence that Stender’s *Truth of Religion* <note>original: "Stenders Wahrheit der Religion." Gotthard Friedrich Stender (1714–1796) was a Baltic German clergyman and polymath. His work sought to defend religious faith against the rising tide of skepticism by finding divine purpose in nature.</note> was bound to have on the ideas of Cedrinus, given the circumstances under which he read this text and in connection with the activities to which he now abandoned himself.

The book could perhaps most briefly be called a popular teleology <term>teleology: the philosophical attempt to explain the universe in terms of design or purpose, often arguing that the complexity of nature proves the existence of a Creator</term>, if that expression were sufficiently intelligible to the common reader. It possesses all the weaknesses that are inseparable from a teleology that seeks to win over common sense more than to instruct it. However, it becomes a true manual for religious enthusiasm <term>Schwärmerey: a pejorative Enlightenment-era term for fanaticism, mysticism, or excessive emotionalism in religion</term> by substituting imaginations—which the understanding has not refined into poetry—as historical truths for an irritated curiosity. 

Dreams please us in the telling if one knows how to tell them ingeniously; it is then the ideas that occupy us, and we gain even as we are entertained. But the play of the imagination only receives its value from the understanding; and where imagination seeks to obtain by stealth from our inclinations what the understanding has denied it, there begins the

When **Cedrinus** read this, it did not quite occur to him that wisdom which hides its light is not acting wisely when it reproaches those who cannot see it. He merely says, assuming belief in his words as something that goes without saying: "Certainly, majestic Nature—this hand of the **Creator**—has formed humanity for a far more sublime purpose, and allows her secrets to be seen only by the worthy <term>Sons of Wisdom</term> <note>A common esoteric and alchemical term referring to those initiated into higher spiritual or hidden knowledge.</note>; to the unworthy, however, she shows only the shells; and those who are 'strong spirits' only in their imagination are as proud of these as children are of their dolls."

...to be identical to the **Falstaffian** manner, namely: to constantly avoid the obstructive proximity of the opponent.

This principle can, as is well known, be applied in two ways: either by removing oneself from the opponent (which is the **Falstaffian** manner, which has nothing for its justification but instinct) or by using weapons that wound from a distance.

Agility is required for both; but agility in the use of the latter—those weapons fearsome from afar—seems pre-eminently to be one of the secret arts of the **warriors for the light** <note>original: *Streiter für das Licht*. This is a sarcastic reference to members of mystical or esoteric societies, such as the Rosicrucians, who claimed to possess "inner light" or hidden divine knowledge.</note>, which shines in the mysteries; an art of which only the application becomes known through its effect.

According to this art, Mr. Stender <note>Gotthard Friedrich Stender (1714–1796), a Baltic German polymath and theologian. The author is critiquing Stender’s later mystical writings.</note> also seeks—as soon as he has triumphed over the simple-minded **freethinkers** <note>original: *Freygeister*. During the 18th century, this term was often used pejoratively by the orthodox or mystics to describe rationalists who questioned religious dogma.</note> through the dark suggestion of the mysteries of the worthy **sons of wisdom**—to remove these shamed individuals far, far away from the whole circle of those who approach him, and from whom he apparently no longer wishes to withhold what they have ears to hear <note>A biblical allusion to the words of Jesus (e.g., Matthew 11:15), here used ironically to describe Stender's selective "revelations."</note>.

What had been praised with such magnificent words in that assembly speech of the <term>R. C. <note>original: *R. C.* Abbreviation for the Rosicrucians (*Rosae Crucis*), a mystical secret society that claimed to possess ancient esoteric knowledge and spiritual wisdom.</note></term> of the old system was asserted here by a clergyman of our church—one who had identified himself and shared so much of his history with such obliging frankness—with more modest expressions, cautiously as it were, but with no less confidence. — It would have required a great deal of specialized knowledge for a man like him, whose nature was not inclined toward distrust, to have become suspicious based on his experiences at the time.

All these circumstances were necessary—not to win Cedrinus over to this incomprehensible preacher of secret wisdom, but so that he might simply set aside the incomprehensible and not find the striking parts offensive.

One becomes a very patient reader as soon as one has grown accustomed to the author's tone; and what one would laugh at on the first page is already tolerable by the tenth, provided that what came before was entertaining or pleasing.

On the hundredth page, under the heading: **On the Principles of Nature-Wisdom** <note>original: *Naturweisheit*. ...without any hesitation, "God makes use of this path of **revelation** <term>revelation <note>original: *Offenbarung*. Cedrinus had never had great experiences with dreams, much less speculated on the theory of dreams. This field of human knowledge was unknown to him, and he read what stood written before him without further reflections, in that state of mind which one might call **tranquility of spirit**, in which we neither assert nor accept anything, but are nonetheless somewhat closer to belief than to unbelief.

and the more difficult this became for him, the more zealously he continued. He believed that the fault lay with him.

There exists a certain talent for talkativeness <note>original: *Redseligkeit*. The author is describing a type of superficial eloquence or "gift of gab" that substitutes complex-sounding words for actual understanding.</note> that seduces nine out of ten people—scholars and laypeople alike—into granting themselves and others more credit in the business of exchanging concepts than there is actual capital. In this trade, one is less on guard against recklessness than in monetary transactions. The ready speaker uses words as the merchant uses paper money, which he only rejects if he fears he cannot spend it again. In this way, conversation gains momentum, just as circulation benefits from bank notes. But the unpleasant consequences are the same in both cases as soon as real value is lacking, which sooner or later must cause its artificial token to dissolve. Only, we are more likely to forgive one another for empty concepts than for money, and more readily accept a few hollow words than invalid currency! —

Had **Cedrinus** possessed such a talent and only had the goodwill to cultivate it, Mr. **Stender’s** <note>Gotthard Friedrich Stender (1714–1796) was a Baltic German author and theologian. Readers will remember that **Cedrinus** had already been directed once—specifically during the assembly speech of the R. C. <note>An abbreviation for the Rosicrucians (*Rosae Crucis*), a mystical secret society that rose to prominence in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note> of the old system <note>The "Old System" likely refers to the "Gold and Rosy Cross," a conservative, highly structured Masonic-Rosicrucian order active in the late 18th century.</note>—to the story of creation and the wisdom of Solomon <note>In esoteric traditions, King Solomon was revered as the ultimate master of divine, natural, and magical secrets.</note>. Cedrinus could not easily conclude from what he was reading that the confidence with which his author spoke was, in fact, a consequence of that audacity which more commonly accompanies ignorance than scholarship. He was unfamiliar with the works of the ancient sages to whom his author referred; and since none of these sages were called by name, nor were their works cited by their specific titles, any inquiry with others was made very difficult for him—even if such an inquiry had been permitted. He had never been educated in wordplay or ringing rhetorical exercises; thus, the pleasure that some people derive from the mere skill of connecting words with words was lost on him. Even this rich source of self-deception could offer him no substitute for the uncomfortable awareness of his own lack of clear concepts. He read, in truth, to understand what he was reading;

Perhaps what we have just described as a possible career toward fame for Cedrinus is, in fact, the secret history of many a highly revered apostle of secret wisdom. People are not so wicked that they would find pleasure in deceiving one another with full awareness of the deceit—especially since this business is not among the easiest, and possesses the particular disadvantage of becoming harder and more burdensome for the one practicing it in the same proportion that the awareness of the effort he is making becomes more vivid, present, and clear within himself. One comes to recognize the cunning within oneself, and the cunning man judges others by himself just as much as the simple man does; however, the former has more to fear from those who know the truth than the latter. 

— When one recalls this observation, for which common life provides daily cause, even such calculated and artificially measured systems of deceit as the one into which Cedrinus had now fallen—and whose inner connection we endeavor to present to our readers through his story—take on a more human appearance, and seem—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the psychology of deception. The previous page suggested that systems of deceit, like the one Cedrinus encountered, appear more human when we realize they are often driven by common human failings rather than pure villainy, and seem—</meta>

—not so much like entirely incomprehensible snares of an unnatural malice.

Some people are valued for their particular natural virtues, and these individuals are usually the least conscious of them. Others wish to assert themselves. Their number is just as great as the variety of means by which one acquires a worth that depends solely on the opinion of others—often on their lack of understanding—and never on ourselves. Since these people, who essentially live for their appearance in the eyes of others, have neither the time nor the ability to take an interest in the general affairs of humanity (as great men do) and do not possess enough simplicity <note>The author uses "simplicity" (Simplicität) here to mean a natural, honest clarity of mind and a lack of pretension.</note> to see with their own eyes as every honest person who follows nature does, it would be a miracle if their path were to avoid injustice, or if they were to recognize a falsehood which their convenience <note>The original term "Convenienz" refers to what is socially advantageous or personally suitable for maintaining one's status.</note> requires them to accept as truth. Gradually, as they continue to gain prestige and influence in an improper manner, it also becomes convenient for them to close their own eyes and to blind the eyes of others. And often, what we take for a work of cunning, malice, or stupidity happens merely for the sake of self-preservation.

Providence <term>Vorsehung; the concept of divine guidance or intervention in the world</term> received these revelations for the salvation of suffering humanity! — This story is insignificant; but because, alas! people cannot grasp the incomprehensible: he merely throws this insignificant story at them, which they do not dare to deny; for it is indeed cited from the undeniable records of the Danish court <note>The author refers to the historical accounts of Corfitz Ulfeldt, a 17th-century Danish nobleman whose life was filled with scandal and intrigue</note> and cited by a famous historian! It serves as a single example out of many. It is not much more than what happens every day, if only one were always to hear of it. And basically, any fairy tale could be used instead of this one. A small twist can grant this usefulness to any old wives' tale <note>original: "Ammengeschichte," literally a story told by a wet-nurse to children</note>; and indeed, a hundred sad farces <term>Possen; absurd or ridiculous tricks, here referring to superstitious myths</term> have been elevated to truths in the imaginations of a thousand people through evidence that was no better.

One believes they can conclude something from such a little story, and imagines that they must accept this conclusion because they cannot deny the fact <note>original: "Factum"</note> within the narrative. But this is a mere empty deception based on a rhetorical trick, which is little suited to serving the truth.

Conversely, one should conclude: because that which is inferred from the narrative is unbelievable, the narrative itself deserves no belief. And whoever has merely grown accustomed to this cautious way of drawing conclusions will very soon...

They study the phenomena <note>German: *Erscheinungen*. In philosophical terms, these are things as they appear to our senses, rather than as they might exist in themselves.</note>, and as they become somewhat acquainted with the unknown forces of nature—because they recognize, compare, investigate, and partially fathom their effects—they also learn to more clearly understand the human faculties that they apply so purposefully. Whatever then remains for them as unknown land, they nevertheless observe its boundaries all the more carefully, as far as their view is able to carry a steady gaze. Limited as our abilities are, it is already a gain for the human spirit to perceive where our knowledge ends, and to practice ourselves in that so necessary skill of distinguishing, in occurring cases, with a confidence that depends solely on a completely dispassionate precision, what is poetry, imagination, delusion, or insight. — The greatest men in the sciences have become great because, from early on, they had a natural need to give themselves a strict account of their own thoughts. Hyperphysics <term>Hyperphysik: Literally "above physics." In the 18th century, this referred to speculative theories that relied on mystical or metaphysical guesses rather than physical evidence.</term> proceeds in a completely different way. It loves enthusiasm, fancies, and dreams. When Newton <note>Isaac Newton (1642–1727), whose work on light and prisms proved that white light is composed of a spectrum of colors.</note> learned to understand the nature of the rainbow by researching the refraction of light rays, and found that colors depend on the shape of the uncolored glass <note>A reference to a glass prism.</note> and that our eye only sees what would not be there without this eye: then the hyperphysicists, by a shorter path, make this eye—which is so easily deceived—into an infallible judge, and elevate our fancies to the status of insight. — It requires no great art to persuade us of something that already appears to us as such anyway. But we also gain no knowledge of nature when we wander about with our imaginations in a world that we have created ourselves. That we find ourselves familiar there is no miracle; this acquaintance is nothing other than the memory of our own dreams.

The naturalist <note>German: *Naturforscher*. Literally "nature-searcher," a term for those we would now call scientists.</note> seeks to recognize the nature of fire as clearly as he can. There is a difference that must be obvious to everyone: the honest naturalist <note>German: *Naturforscher*. A term used before the word "scientist" was coined to describe those who studied the physical world through observation.</note>, who makes no claim to knowledge beyond the limits of human experience and judgment, does everything possible to subject his experiments to scrutiny. In contrast, the hyperphysicist <term>Hyperphysiker: A practitioner of "hyperphysics," a speculative field that relied on mystical or metaphysical guesses rather than physical evidence.</term> makes it almost the first requirement for his students that he be allowed to make arbitrary demands upon their faith. The former loves science more than his own reputation; the latter uses the public’s opinion of science merely to gain a hearing. He needs to know little or nothing, provided he can make many words. What he presents will always have an effect because it is striking, and it will occupy the mind all the more the less it provides true satisfaction. Humans soon grasp the comprehensible and are done with it; but they have tortured their intellects for centuries over incomprehensibilities.

The reading into which Cedrinus <note>A character in this narrative, likely a student or seeker of knowledge.</note> had now fallen through higher guidance had piqued his expectations enough to seek satisfaction in the instruction of the highly-reverend Superior <note>German: *Vorsteher*. This could refer to the head of a school, a monastery, or a specific religious community.</note>, and had shown his imagination such unlimited fields of the knowable that he was perfectly prepared to be a patient listener, regardless of how the lecture might turn out.

In such circles, passing judgment is a sin. Secret wisdom, much like those banks whose "treasure" is merely their secrecy, must maintain its credit by trying to extend the circulation of its notes for as long as possible. No consideration is given to who loses in the end. curiosity—to say nothing of the impropriety of asking questions in a society where everyone’s instruction is handed down from above—he would have gained nothing but the disadvantage of exposing his own inability to grasp the incomprehensible. Nothing was more natural, therefore, than for no one to ask. Moreover, in such societies, everyone looks into the eyes of his neighbor as if he perfectly understood that which the other cannot grasp. And everyone can maintain this appearance in the most advantageous way in the world; for no one is exposed to the unpleasantness of being put to the test. Who would want to question another, since all are perfectly equal in two respects: their shared ignorance and their equally shared dependence on their superiors?

The ancient poets, out of a natural dread of contradictions, left chaos as chaos. The "higher wisdom" presented by the Reverend <note>original: *Hochwürdige*. Yet it was claimed that God had made this division; and indeed, this was the best means of raising it above all criticism.

<page-num>181</page-num>
<sig>M 5</sig>

The upper part of the chaos was supposed to be completely illuminated and extremely subtle. — Indeed, they were not very concerned with observing the distinction of time regarding the chaos. They also spoke of the non-entity <note>original: *Unding*. Literally "non-thing," this term refers to an absurdity or a conceptual monstrosity—something that cannot logically exist.</note> that was supposedly transformed into a world through creation as if it still existed as a non-entity. — The lower chaos, on the other hand, is according to this theory perfectly dark, coarse, thick, and impure. The middle chaos has something of each of its two neighbors.

Nevertheless, it was taught regarding this very coarse, thick, impure, and dark lower chaos that it contained all properties, virtues, powers, and essences of the creatures of the first class <note>Likely referring to primordial spiritual beings or the first stage of created existence in this mystical system.</note> in manifest form, and thus contained formal light, formal delicacy, and purity itself. And what the creatures in this lower chaos actually are in manifest form, the upper creatures in the upper chaos were only supposed to be in potential power and hidden essence.

Nonsense! one will say. Manifest nonsense! An upper chaos that is subtle, and a lower chaos that is coarse! The upper chaos is supposed to be the better one, and yet the lower chaos has everything that the upper chaos has, and something more besides! And the middle chaos has what they both have!

**Vocabulary:** 
Chaos, Creation, Non-entity, Lower Chaos, Middle Chaos, Upper Chaos, Creatures, Essences, Potential Power

In this process, darkness always remains darkness, and light always remains light. However, according to the theory of the **Theoretical Brethren of Solomonic Wisdom**, it is absolutely necessary for light to reveal itself in darkness, subtlety in coarseness, and power in impotence. Thus, this difficult business is at least made easier if the darkness already contains light, the coarseness already contains subtlety, and the impotence already contains power.

That a man of sound mind like *Cedrinus*, who does not share this need for lecturing and can in no way flatter himself with its satisfaction, could nevertheless summon enough patience to master the situation—as was required to have the chaotic notebooks of the <term>Superior</term> <note>original: *Obervorsteher*. But yet, this phenomenon might be easily enough explained by the human capacity for expectation. In this distressing but deeply preoccupying state, our eyes are fixed too intently on that which exists nowhere but in our restless imagination to perceive what is actually happening around us and to us. What we do then, we do in a kind of absence of mind, with little more consciousness than in a dream.

## Chapter Five.

### Continuation of the Same Matter.

---

When the small pamphlet containing the <term>theoretical degree</term> <note>The *Theoretischer Grad* was the junior level of the "Gold- and Rosicrucians," focusing on a mix of alchemy and mystical theology.</note> of the **Rosicrucians** had appeared in print, the <term>Superior</term> <note>original: *Obervorsteher*. The presiding officer or Master of the local Rosicrucian circle.</note> entered the assembly with a troubled face and anxious expressions. With deep sighs, he announced to the brothers present "that a traitor to the society had published the entire degree in print; because of which he, the Superior, had received an order from the <term>High Superiors</term> <note>original: *hohen Obern*. The shadowy, supposedly enlightened leaders who governed the Rosicrucian Order from afar, often remaining anonymous even to high-ranking members.</note> not to open or close the assembly according to the previous **ritual** henceforth, until the High Superiors had first made other arrangements that had now become necessary."

For nearly half a year, the meetings were thus continued in a quiet and solemn fashion, but without those **ceremonies** which our readers learned of at the end of the first book. Meanwhile, the most reverend Superior did not interrupt his <term>hyperphysical-mystical</term> <note>A term used in the 18th century to describe occult theories that claimed to explain the spiritual forces behind physical nature.</note> lectures; and finally, he also resumed the old customs, explaining "that the High Superiors now permitted such things again, because they had recognized that from that treach—" <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "räthe-" indicates the word is "verrätherischen" (treacherous/traitorous).</note>

Cedrinus did not yet have enough general knowledge at that time of the nature of the <term>mysteries</term> <note>In this context, "mysteries" refers to the secret rituals and esoteric traditions of both ancient civilizations and modern secret societies.</note> and the history of all secret societies to make the observations regarding this explanation that a less personally invested observer might have been prompted to make. Since then, his experience has sufficiently taught him that the anxieties of the <term>High Superiors</term> <note>original: *hohen Obern*. The anonymous, high-ranking leaders of the Order.</note> and the most reverend Superior had been, to a high degree, in vain. — A secret society whose ceremonies, through an unfortunate intervention, are surrendered to the publicity of the printing press, has nothing more nor less to do than to continue on quite undisturbed. As long as its members themselves are not incautious enough to publicly acknowledge the authenticity of such documents, the public becomes confused, soon forgets what it has read, and develops a mistrust toward writings that claim to betray secrets; it would have to be a quite extraordinary misfortune if the secret society did not outlive its own description. Cedrinus would have soon found this to be true in his own experience, had it been gold that occupied his thoughts. With his healthy eyes, he would have very quickly discovered the "nothingness" that stands in for this goal. But this did not entice his desires, and for that reason, he overlooked it—no matter how closely it lay before his eyes—for as long as it was possible to overlook it.

It was knowledge, universal knowledge, or rather the mere, empty concept of a universal knowledge of all that exists—a concept as boundless as it was vacant—that kept his activity stirred. And where, among all the dreams that have gained the appearance of a science by taking the form of a system, is there one that promises more satisfaction to the still undefined drive for a universal knowledge of beings than <term>Theosophy</term>? <note>original: *Theosophie*. A mystical philosophy seeking direct knowledge of the divine and the hidden secrets of nature through spiritual intuition.</note> This was the magic to which Cedrinus, in hope and patience, surrendered himself more and more completely.

It is almighty. It can unite contradictions as soon as it has made itself independent of the sovereignty of thought.

### Sixth Chapter.

#### Johann Gottfried Jugel.

Jugel's writings are diverse, and it seems as if the man took great pains toward the knowledge of nature without noticing himself how often, in his investigations, playful wit <note>The German "Witz" in this period refers to an intellectual cleverness or the faculty of making quick, often superficial associations, rather than just humor.</note> took the place of calm judgment; whereby self-love, which prefers to teach rather than to learn, has perhaps been most detrimental to him. — To overlook the whole of nature, as it were, from a single point of view, requires a great spirit served by a lively imagination, but who is conscious of what the imagination is doing during every activity of his fancy, and therefore never confuses that which he only imagines with that which all people can experience. But he who creates an image of nature in his imagination, and is weak enough to believe in its existence, can only too easily attain enough wit (for wit also increases through practice)

<summary>This page concludes a critique of Cedrinus and begins a new chapter on Johann Gottfried Jugel, criticizing Jugel for allowing imagination and "wit" to override empirical observation and calm judgment in his natural philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Gottfried Jugel, natural philosophy, imagination, wit, self-love, epistemology, alchemy, 18th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Johann Gottfried Jugel, picking up from the previous page's discussion on how practice in "wit" or intellectual cleverness allows a person to construct imaginary systems of nature.</meta>

to explain all experiences according to his own imagination.

For how does one explain things, once one is so bribed or blinded? — Mostly through a play with concepts, where none is clearly defined or fully explored, and often through an obvious, flat wordplay that would provoke laughter if the tone were less serious.

In fact, nothing is more common in the writings of the theosophists <note>In the 18th-century context, "theosophists" refers to mystical philosophers who claimed direct, divinely inspired knowledge of nature and the divine, often through alchemical or esoteric means.</note> than such wordplays. One never pursues a concept to the point of clarity, because an obscure concept has the advantage of being interchangeable with many others without anyone noticing, provided no other name is given to it. In this way, theories can be designed without principles, and these systems of doctrine <term>Lehrgebäude: Literally an "educational building," referring to a structured, systematic body of teachings.</term> then resemble a fairy-castle <note>German: "Feenschlosse," implying a structure that is beautiful but lacks any foundation in reality.</note> that can be imagined anywhere, because the relationship of the foundation to the building is always assumed, yet a blueprint or scale is never provided. — One could call theosophy a poor kind of poetry if it were only content to be an art. But it insists on believing its own inspirations in earnest and demands they be believed by others. Because it cannot provide proof, the authority of a revelation is indispensable to its pronouncements.

Mr. Jugel also calls his physics a revelation. It is one of his principal books, which he [published] as late as the year 1782 in Berlin

by George Jacob Decker under the title: “Johann Gottfried Jugel’s **PHYSICA MYSTICA** and **PHYSICA SACRA SACRATISSIMA** <note>original: "PHYSICA MYSTICA und PHYSICA SACRA SACRATISSIMA." The addition “To the Praise and Glory of the Most High One God” does about as much to help the content along in certain books as the notice in other writings that they are to be sold for the benefit of the poor, or for a family that has lost their home to fire. One usually picks up the latter not without compassion; and although this compassion is actually intended only for the unfortunate family, it almost always has such a softening influence on our criticism that the charitable writer essentially owes as much gratitude to the poor as they owe to him. In the same way, an author who announces himself as a man who writes for the glory of God easily places us in a certain serious mood, in which expectation and modesty unite too closely for us not to unnoticedly yield to the <note>The text cuts off here, leading into the catchword "Mans," likely beginning a sentence about yielding to the "man's" authority or persuasion.</note>

### Chapter Seven.

### Johann Gottfried Jugel’s Anthropology

There are three things—for even if there were more, how could I now, so deeply entangled in the secrets of the holy number three, think of anything else but three?—there are three things where man must entirely (however incomprehensible and wonderful this high mystery seems to be) forget his own self and, however one might please to call it, deny himself or "crucify his flesh": heroism, love, and philosophy.

### Johann Gottfried Jugel’s Anthropology

Just as little as a "two-penny hero" <note>original: *Zwey-Groschen-Held*; a derogatory term for a cheap mercenary or a cowardly person posing as a soldier</note> will learn to perform a great deed, even if he has spent three days running through the streets; and just as little as the fop—who finds a mirror in every beautiful eye to admire his own charming features—will ever learn to love; just as little will the vain pedant ever think or discover truths. He sits like a spider in the middle of the web it has spun to ensnare innocent flies, making his own petty personality into a sort of **world-soul** <term>World-soul: the *anima mundi*, a philosophical concept suggesting a spiritual force that animates the entire universe</term> and placing himself at the center, so that all threads of the great whole (at least as far as his sphere of influence reaches) emanate from him and are drawn back to him. — The less he is aware of this himself, the worse it is. **Egoism is the consumption of the soul;** <note>original: *Schwindsucht der Seele*. --- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of intellectual vanity, shifting from the pedant's "world-soul" to a scathing takedown of the "Theosophists"—mystical philosophers who claim direct knowledge of the divine. It picks up from the previous discussion of egoism as a "consumption of the soul."</meta>

There are no more selfish people in the world than the **Theosophists**. <term>Theosophists: in this 18th-century context, these are mystical philosophers (such as followers of Jakob Böhme) who claimed to gain knowledge of the divine through spiritual ecstasy or direct intuition.</term> Into everything they see or hear—or even what they neither see nor hear, but merely dream of—they project their own petty little soul: into God, just as into a lump of ore or a dung beetle. No wonder they are thus everywhere and nowhere at home! — A Theosophist, with the rapid-fire alternation of his dreams, is one moment a flea on the dirty fur of a shepherd’s dog and the next an angel before the shining throne of the **World-Creator**. He therefore believes he knows the secrets of everyone and measures the dimensions of the universe by the same scale as the steps of a flea.

For this reason, all the theories of the Theosophists seem to have such a human appearance. At their core, however, they are nothing but confusion expressed through human tones; and only because no actual concepts lie at their foundation does one allow oneself to be babbled at for a while. Happy is the man who only listens and, not overcome by the sleep-inducing melody, begins to dream along with them!

How well **Mr. Jugel** <note>Johann Gottfried Jugel (1707–1791) was a German author known for his many works on alchemy, mining, and mystical natural philosophy.</note> knows how to explain man! — Everything in him is pure **magnet**, <term>Magnet: likely a reference to the alchemical or "vitalist" idea of a universal force or "animal magnetism" that connects all things.</term> coagulated and uncoagulated magnet, body-magnet and soul-magnet!

"Light and darkness," he says in the thirty-fifth paragraph, "are the two emanations of eternity," — — "from which love now arises..."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Johann Gottfried Jugel’s theories on the human "magnet." Love and Anger, as the two offspring of the Sideric influence <note>Sideric: relating to the stars or celestial bodies. In alchemical and mystical thought, the "sideric body" is the astral link between the physical body and the soul.</note>, wherein and whereby the elements stand ready to accomplish everything according to this stated influence. We find in human affairs that all our conduct originates from these two general emanations of eternity—to a greater or lesser degree, according to what the *Astra* <term>Astra: Latin for "stars." The author refers to the celestial forces or "influences" believed to govern human temperament and fate.</term> may permit at various times; this, then, is called nature, which has been incorporated into man during his generation <note>In this context, "generation" refers to the process of conception and formation in the womb.</note>. Nevertheless, the magnet governs here with its power of attraction for the maintenance of the body, which is at all times sustained from the Sideric realm through constant breathing. 

In love and sorrow—that is, in the mixing of anger and love—consist our daily occupations. These two natural essences, implanted within us in the earthly realm, are often stirred in us humans in an unexpected manner, as their fundamental basis is quite easily excited and moved by the second, incomprehensible magnet. This magnet is quite distinct from that which the upper astral sustaining power constantly instills in us through breathing, and it cannot be brought to any visible recognition, except that through its effect—when it is excited in a magnetic fashion—the sensory essence in which it "imagines" <note>original: *immaginiret*. ...is formed, which then usually breaks out in love and friendliness, or in hatred and anger!”

Nonsense! Nonsense! exclaims the reader who has had patience until now. Nonsense, in which there is not even a method!

But what kind of magnet can this second “incomprehensible magnet” be, which “cannot be brought to any visible recognition” and is “imagined in the sensory essence”?

What other kind than—**Imagination**?

The power of imagination! The power of imagination is this magnet!—O Aristotle, Locke, Hume, Helvétius, Reimarus, Kant, Platner, Reinhold <note>The author lists a "Who's Who" of Enlightenment and Classical philosophers to mock Jugel's obscure mysticism by comparison.</note>, all you men who have ever troubled your uninspired reason with concepts, step forward before the lecturer's chair of the anthropologist of all anthropologists, the great **Johann Gottfried Jugel**, and learn to use words to build theories!—Thus speaks the wisdom of **Johann Gottfried Jugel**:

“This magnet thus described now manifests itself in two kinds of essences: it lies in salt” <note>In alchemy, "Salt" is one of the three primary principles (Tria Prima) along with Sulfur and Mercury, representing the body or the principle of fixation.</note>—the poor thing! ...to draw the **power of imagination** to itself, and usually carries out the resulting deeds—whether they be good or evil—in an often completely reckless manner. In these last words, we finally have an explanation of magic from a theosophist! <note>In this context, a "theosophist" refers to someone claiming direct divine illumination or speculative mystical knowledge of God and nature, such as Jakob Böhme or, in this case, Jugel.</note>

Children and—enthusiasts speak the truth precisely because they do not know it themselves. Yes, indeed, magic is an exercise of the **imagination**! Regarding nothing else in this entire book will sober-minded people agree so much with Mr. Jugel as on this truth, which slips out from him in the midst of his enthusiasm.

Anyone who has read several theosophical writings must have long ago noticed that all the systems of these "inspired" people—whether from ancient or modern times, from the <term>Orient: Historically "the East," referring to Middle Eastern or Asian cultures.</term> or the <term>Occident: Historically "the West," referring to Europe and the Americas.</term>—resemble one another in one point. This point is a very important point. They all lack **freedom**. They do not even have an inkling of the concept of freedom. Even the pious enthusiasts <note>German: *Schwärmer*; in the 18th century, this was a derogatory term for religious fanatics or those who claimed to be guided by private "inner light" rather than reason.</note> and female enthusiasts, who could not quite rise—or go astray—into actual <term>theosophy: In this context, a form of mystical or esoteric speculation that seeks knowledge of God through nature, often linked to alchemy.</term>, always began by denying the freedom of man. All religious fanaticism <note>German: *Schwärmerey*</note> originates from the degradation of human nature.

According to the <term>anthropology: The philosophical study of the nature of the human soul and body.</term> of any theosophist, all morality falls away. Furthermore, morality is basically as incompatible with theosophy as it is with <term>theocracy: A system of government where God is the immediate ruler; the author implies that if God controls everything directly, human moral choice is impossible.</term>.

The theosophist is the universal Cyclops of nature. Under his hands, everything turns into metal and ore. He forges thunderbolts or chains—it is all the same! To him, God is the being who makes metals; and he wants to learn from Him how to make metals. This is his "divine resemblance," this is his refinement of human nature. It has cast many people deep, deep down.

When the noble poet elevates beings that have no life for us into persons: he breathes his own creative soul into their forms, and it is our life, like our image, that smiles back at us from this new creation. Thus, through his inspiration, the whole world becomes a quintessence of moral beings. The force of attraction, which the physicist has discovered, clothes

The **refinement** <note>German: *Veredlung*; in alchemy, this refers to the "ennobling" of base metals into gold.</note> which the theosophist wishes to bestow upon human nature is **gold-making**. # First Chapter.

### Cedrinus Becomes a Rosicrucian.

For almost a year, the kind of reading we observed in the previous book occupied the mind of **Cedrinus** at home, while in the meetings, he listened to the lectures of the Right Reverend <note>original: "Hochwürdigen" — likely referring to a high-ranking official or "Venerable Master" within the lodge</note>. — Nonsense costs no less time than the investigation of truth; likewise, nonsense reveals no fewer facets to the observer than truth does. For the student, each of these facets represents a new lesson.

In November of the year 1784, Brother N— came to him and said, with a tone of meritorious sympathy: "he had been so fortunate as to obtain permission from the High Superiors <note>original: "hohen Obern" — the secret leadership common in 18th-century occult orders</note> for **Cedrinus** to advance further within the Order and be initiated more deeply into the mysteries." And before **Cedrinus** had even found time to respond, he added with a look of great earnestness and a half-warning tone, "but this is a very important step! He who has once taken it can never go back. No worldly power can release someone from the obligations they would undertake! He should therefore reflect maturely and seriously before he decides."

<term>Cedrinus</term> <term>Rosicrucian</term> <term>Order</term> <term>Mysteries</term> <term>Initiation</term> <term>Brother N—</term>

<summary>Cedrinus is invited by a fellow member to advance deeper into the secrets of the Rosicrucian Order, receiving a solemn warning about the irreversible nature of the initiation vows.</summary>
<keywords>Cedrinus, Rosicrucians, secret societies, initiation, 1784, Brother N—, occultism, Golden and Rosy Cross</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>The text follows from the previous page where Brother N— warned Cedrinus that his decision to advance in the Order would be irreversible. This page explores the psychological effect of that warning and records Cedrinus's formal petition for membership.</meta>

There are no more effective enticements than when one pretends to deter someone in such a way that their courage is put to the test. A person then takes sides against themselves and against their own reason; folly thereby imperceptibly becomes a matter of honor.

**Cedrinus** replied to the friend—who was so kind and yet so cautious, so communicative and yet so reserved—that he had confidence in him that the matter would contain nothing evil or in any way harmful. He also hoped that there would be no costs associated with it that might exceed his means. — Brother **N**— said with reassuring gravity: "If there were the slightest evil or disadvantage in the whole matter, his conscience would forbid him from ever having said a word to him about it."

Now **Cedrinus** had no further hesitations; and **N**— instructed him to draft a very short but indeed powerful petition: "I, J. F. R..., Master of the Radiance of Light <note>original: "Meister vom Scheine des Lichtes" — likely a specific rank or degree held by Cedrinus within his current lodge</note> and the Lost Word <note>The "Lost Word" is a central motif in Masonic and occult traditions, symbolizing a divine truth or secret knowledge that was lost to humanity and must be recovered through initiation</note>, most humbly and obediently pray that the grace be shown to me to be admitted into the most holy Order of the Gold and Rosy Cross of the Old System <note>The "Gold- und Rosenkreuzer" was a highly influential secret society in the 1780s that blended alchemy, mysticism, and conservative politics</note>."

<summary>Driven by a sense of honor and reassured by Brother N— that the initiation carries no risk, Cedrinus submits a formal petition to join the higher ranks of the Gold and Rosy Cross.</summary>

<keywords>Cedrinus, Brother N, secret societies, initiation, petition, Gold and Rosy Cross, Lost Word, psychological manipulation, 18th-century occultism</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: Cedrinus has just drafted his formal petition to join the Order of the Gold and Rosy Cross, despite the weight of the commitment. This page describes the period of spiritual preparation required of him and the summons to his initiation site.</meta>

Even the words grace, humility, and obedience, which appeared in the form, did not deter **Cedrinus**. He wrote exactly as **N—** dictated to him. The note received no address. **N—** promised to deliver it to the proper place.

About a month later, **Brother N—** appeared again and informed **Cedrinus** that he had now received permission to bring him closer to the Order. It was to take place in a few days. The day, place, and hour were not yet determined. But with great emphasis, **Brother N—** exhorted **Cedrinus** to use the time leading up to that important event for a most careful and conscientious preparation, so that he might be worthy of what was to happen to him. He recommended that he avoid all worldly amusements, advised him to consume no strong drink during this time, and to also deny himself those sensual pleasures permitted by marriage <note>The Order required a period of ascetic purification, common in high-degree Masonry and Rosicrucianism, to "cleanse" the candidate's spirit before receiving higher mysteries</note>.

After a few days, he received a written summons in which **N—** invited him to come at six o'clock in the afternoon to the Right Reverend \*\*\*'s garden in the **Fühlentwiete** <note>Fühlentwiete is a historic street in Hamburg, historically associated with various lodge meeting places and private gardens</note>, where the serving brother \*\*\* would show him to a room in which he should remain until **N—** himself appeared to instruct him further.

<summary>Following his petition, Cedrinus undergoes a month of ascetic preparation, including fasting and abstinence, before receiving a formal summons to an initiation site in a Hamburg garden.</summary>
<keywords>initiation, asceticism, purification, Hamburg, Fühlentwiete, Gold and Rosy Cross, Brother N—</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: Cedrinus has arrived at the designated garden in the Fühlentwiete as instructed, having completed his month of purification and ascetic preparation. He is now waiting for Brother N— to begin the formal initiation ceremony into the Order.</meta>

**Cedrinus** did not waste any time. Having prepared conscientiously, he arrived at the appointed place.

He had not waited long when **Brother N—** appeared in the regalia <note>The term "Ornat" refers to the formal ritual vestments or robes worn by members of secret societies during ceremonies</note> of a Companion of **Salomonic Wisdom** <note>The "Salomonic Wisdom" likely refers to a specific branch or high degree of the Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross, which claimed to possess the lost wisdom of King Solomon</note>. He asked him whether he was still minded to connect himself more closely with the **Order**, and he repeated his warnings as well as his assurances that once someone had taken this decisive step, neither divine nor human power could release him from the duties that would be laid upon him. — **Cedrinus** answered just as he had answered four weeks earlier in his own house.

**Brother N—** then led **Cedrinus** into another room, locked it, and commanded him to wash his hands. Thereupon he handed him an apron of **Salomonic Wisdom**, with which **Cedrinus** girded himself. The Brother, however, bound his hands together with a red cord and covered his eyes with a white cloth <note>The blindfold, or "hoodwink," and the binding of hands are traditional symbols in initiatory rites, representing the candidate's state of spiritual darkness and his total submission to the Order before being "born again" into the light</note>. Thus bound, and with a bared flaming sword in his hand, the Brother led him to the door of the next room. He knocked softly nine times. From within, there was one return knock. Then the door opened, and one of the brothers asked from within: "Who is there?" **N—** answered: "An earthly body, which desires to be transformed into a spiritual one." — — There followed several—

<summary>Cedrinus undergoes the first stage of a ritual initiation, involving symbolic washing, donning a ritual apron, and being blindfolded and bound before entering the inner sanctum of the Order.</summary>

Questions and answers, which **Cedrinus** did not retain precisely enough.

Finally, **Brother N—** led him inside. — He was asked the following question: “Are you determined to unite yourself more closely with us, in order to attain wisdom, art, and virtue, to please God, and to serve your neighbor?” — — By his voice, **Cedrinus** recognized the Right Worshipful **Presiding Superior** <note>The "Ober-Vorsteher" or Presiding Superior acts as the master of the lodge or circle during the ceremony</note> of the Brothers of **Salomonic Wisdom**. When he had answered “Yes,” this loquacious man delivered a long speech, in which he impressed upon him with great gravity <note>original: "Gravität"; referring to a solemn and dignified manner</note> the duties that **Cedrinus** was now pledging himself to fulfill; duties that, in holiness and importance, surpassed everything else usually called duty. Thereupon, he ordered his hands to be untied and the blindfold to be removed from his eyes.

Now **Cedrinus** found himself in a spacious room, illuminated by a chandelier hanging in the center. On the floor before him lay a small rectangular carpet <note>In initiatory traditions, a "carpet" or tracing board is a cloth or board painted with the symbols of the specific rank or degree to serve as a visual aid for the candidate's instruction</note>, upon which four circles, or rings, were drawn one inside the other; a red one, a yellow one, a white one, and a black one. Beyond the carpet was a table, draped with a green cloth. On the table, a glass sphere filled with water and painted half black was placed upon a wooden stand. **Cedrinus** had to step into the center of the carpet, where a sphere was painted. As he stood there, the **Circle Director** placed into his hand one of the papers from his table. On it were written, in beautiful Fraktur script <note>original: "Fractur-Schrift"; a ornate, "broken" style of blackletter typeface common in German-speaking lands</note> and very legibly, seven duties, the fulfillment of which

<meta>This page continues the initiation of Cedrinus into the Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross. He is standing in a ritual room before the Circle Director, who has just handed him a document detailing the vows he must take to join the secret society.</meta>

...[obliga]tion <note>The word is partially cut off from the previous page; based on context, it is likely *Verpflichtung* (obligation/oath) or *Handlung* (ceremony)</note> **Cedrinus** was now to swear. He bade him read these through. They consisted of the following points: — to constantly practice the fear of God — never intentionally to offend the love of one's neighbor — to maintain the highest secrecy without fail — to persevere in inviolable loyalty to the Order — to show all obedience to the superiors — not to withhold from the most illustrious brotherhood any secret pertaining to its field, and to live as the property of the Creator, His wisdom, and the Order.

When **Cedrinus** had read through this paper, the **Circle Director** asked him whether he was ready to swear to these seven duties. **Cedrinus** answered without hesitation: “Yes!” Thereupon the **Circle Director** took it back from him, laid it before himself on the table, and held his ash-wood staff, with the A and O <note>original: "A und O"; referring to Alpha and Omega, the first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, symbolizing God as the beginning and the end</note> on both ends, over **Cedrinus’s** head. — Brother N., who had led him in and now stood behind **Cedrinus**, stretched his flaming sword over his head, so that the sword and staff formed an acute angle — and in this manner, he read the oath to him, point by point, very slowly and deliberately. **Cedrinus** repeated it after him just as slowly.

That was the ceremony by which **Cedrinus** became a Disciple (for this degree is called the **Juniorate Degree** <term>Juniorate Degree: The "Juniorats-Grad" was the first and lowest rank within the 18th-century Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross, focusing on preliminary study and obedience</term>) of the Golden and Rosy- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, intended to be *Gold- und Rosenkreuzer* (Golden and Rosy Cross)</note>

<meta>This page continues the initiation of Cedrinus into the Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross. On the previous page, Cedrinus had just sworn his seven-point oath as a Junior, the first degree of the order, while a sword and staff were held over his head.</meta>

...[Junior] <note>completing the word from the previous page: "Juniorat," the lowest rank in the order's hierarchy</note> of the old system.

Now he had to approach the **Circle Director** <term>Circle Director: The "Cirkel-Director" was the local leader of a Rosicrucian "circle," usually consisting of nine members.</term> more closely. He made known to him the sign, grip, and word of this Rosicrucian mystery. — The sign was the posture in which he had already seen the brothers standing: the arms laid crosswise over the chest. Certainly an uncomfortable position! Especially when one must listen to long speeches while in it. But it cannot be denied: it fatigues the body and habituates it to obedience. It is a crucifixion of the bones, even if not of the flesh. — The grip consisted of an extension of the right hand of one toward the right hand of the other, the fingertips turned toward each other, whereupon both hands slid into one another, pressed, and turned thumb against thumb. Also uncomfortable enough! — The word was as follows: “Daleth <note>original: "Dalet"; the fourth letter of the Hebrew alphabet</note>: that is, Urun.” A year later it was changed to: “Daleth; that is, Onego.” <note>These are likely ritual passwords or ciphers used to identify members; their exact linguistic roots are often obscured by the order's internal mystical traditions.</note>

The **Actuarius** <term>Actuarius: The secretary or registrar of the Masonic or Rosicrucian lodge responsible for keeping records.</term> now led **Cedrinus** back into the room where Brother N. had previously bound his hands and veiled his eyes. There he gave him a sheet of paper, upon which **Cedrinus** had to write a short letter of thanks to the high superiors. He did not know a single one of them, and was not made acquainted with any of them here. The inscription of his thanksgiving was: “To a high

## Chapter Two.
### Observations.

While **Cedrinus** fulfills his duties in the side room through these activities, we shall gain time to indulge in some reflections on the—in truth—somewhat reckless trust with which this man, who had been teased for so long, now took on boundless obligations toward people he did not know.

This would not have been possible had not his reading at home and the lectures in the meetings for **Solomonic Wisdom** <note>original: "Salomonische Weisheit"; likely the name of the specific Masonic lodge or Rosicrucian "circle" that Cedrinus attended.</note> constantly dragged his mind and imagination from one point to another over time, until he was, so to speak, in a state of flux between restlessness, curiosity, and

This numbs the soul, just as swinging numbs the senses. <note>The author uses a vivid metaphor here: just as the physical motion of a swing can make a person dizzy and lose their bearings, the psychological manipulation of the initiation process has left Cedrinus's mind spinning and unable to think critically.</note>

Who would not otherwise have been startled, at least by the duty "to conceal no secret from the highly enlightened brotherhood <note>original: "hocherlauchten Verbrüderung"</note> that falls within its scope"!

Can unknown people demand such a thing from an honest man? And can a conscientious man promise such a thing to unknown people? — Who knows what kind of scope these people's business is, and what, according to a definition left entirely to them, might belong in this unknown scope!

But to **Cedrinus**, only the persons of these secret superiors were unknown. Of their virtues, their wisdom, science, and art, a picture had formed in his soul from the obscure ideas induced in so many ways, whose features indicated nothing but excellence and perfection. — Thus one trusts gods who, if not made with hands, are yet made through one's own deceptive art of the imagination! <term>Imagination: In the 18th-century context, "Phantasie" often referred to the mind's ability to create illusions or false realities, rather than just creative thinking.</term>

Meanwhile, <note>This "Meanwhile" (original: "Indeß") serves as the transition from the previous discussion on the psychological trap of the initiation.</note> they are scoundrels who cannot hide their card tricks for even a moment—they promise secrets to honest people who, in good-hearted confidence and with their cash **Louis d'ors** <term>Louis d'ors: French gold coins widely used across Europe in the 18th century as a standard of high value.</term> in hand, come to them. The main objective is to habituate a person to obedience and to make his **Reason**—against which few people here on earth are so hostile as to intentionally take it captive while in a state of full awareness—as useless as possible. For this purpose, deceptive ceremonies, poor writings, and poor lectures are very useful. And to all these means, a certain instinct leads the founders of the Rosicrucian circles of its own accord, without reflection or effort, and just as infallibly as the instinct which leads that well-known animal, which the **people of Jehovah** <note>original: "Volk Jehova’s". Meanwhile, I cannot forbid myself from drawing my readers' attention to this clumsy yet cunning law from another perspective: specifically, regarding the general history of the Rosicrucians.

Upon close examination, it is soon revealed that the current Order of the Rosicrucians is, in fact, very young—much younger than the ancient folly of **gold-making**. <note>The author uses the term "Goldmacherey" to distinguish the primitive, often fraudulent pursuit of transmuting lead into gold from the developing science of chemistry.</note> One can see an unmistakable sign of this in the obligation imposed on members to reveal their secrets.

The ignorant **Secret Superiors** <note>Secret Superiors (Geheime Obern): The alleged hidden leaders of the Rosicrucian order, whose identities were supposedly known only to high-ranking initiates; the author suggests they are frauds who steal knowledge from their subordinates.</note> must seek to recruit members from whom they themselves can learn. This is because, in recent times, **chemistry** has been too effectively developed by philosophical scholars and corrected through the reduction of individual experiments into fundamental principles for the old confusion and chatter about isolated "tricks" to remain valid for much longer. However, for such ignorant people, as unread in the works of scholars as these Superiors are, to realize this required a considerable amount of time. They must have already had many experiences with the inadequacy of their own secrets. 

In the age of **Paracelsus** <term>Paracelsus (1493–1541): A Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine, transitioning away from ancient Greek traditions.</term>, it would never have occurred to any Rosicrucian circle to make the communication of knowledge acquired through one's own industry such a sacred duty. ## Third Chapter.

### Explanation of the Ceremony and the Masonic Hieroglyphs.

The Actuarius <term>Actuarius: An administrative officer or scribe responsible for record-keeping within the Order.</term> led Cedrinus back into the room where the ash staff and the flaming sword had been held over him, and where he had sworn an oath to duties that are so reprehensible before the judgment seat of morality. The first fruit of his actions and suffering was—a coat of arms, neatly painted, which was handed to him by the Circle-Director. Beneath it were the names: Cedrinus, Zechiras, and Edikafron. The Circle-Director stated that “the highest Superiors of the Order had drawn this coat of arms and these names for him by means of the Cabbala. <term>Cabbala: A system of Jewish mysticism; 18th-century secret societies like the Rosicrucians often used its methods of letter-substitution or numerology to create 'magical' or secret names for their members.</term> He was to have the former engraved and make use of it in every letter concerning Order business. From this point forward, he was to bear the name Cedrinus within the Order, but he had to hide the other two names from everyone—even from the brothers themselves—until he received permission from the high Superiors to use them.”

After Cedrinus had received these important jewels from the Order, the enlightened Brother Circle-Director proceeded to explain the figures and images which to the initiate

already known from his first experiences in Freemasonry. <note>The text suggests that the higher-level Rosicrucian symbols being explained were actually derived from or related to the basic symbols used in the lower degrees of Freemasonry.</note>

It will be necessary here to provide the following brief explanation for those among our readers who are familiar with neither books nor their own experiences in the lodges regarding the so-called hieroglyphs—or more correctly, the symbols—of the Masons.

The Masons in England had for a long time kept certain decorations in the rooms of their hostels <note>original: "Herbergen" – meeting places or inns specifically for guild members</note> where the guild members assembled. These consisted mostly of depictions of the tools of the trade, as well as individual parts of a building, fragments, and so forth. These they kept secret among themselves. And if a traveling Mason presented himself to other Masons as a guild brother, he was required—in order to prove his legitimacy—to state what objects he had seen in the Lodge or hostel, in addition to describing the ceremonies performed during his initiation into the guild. He then declared in sequence: "Here stood the column; there lay that trowel, a square," and so on. From this, his genuine membership in the guild was recognized. 

When, approximately in the first quarter of the current century <note>The 1700s, specifically referring to the formation of the Grand Lodge in London in 1717</note>, the Masons' guild in England evolved into a—truthfully idle—fraternity, which, through the honorary membership of several distinguished people...

people, [and] borrowed a vain luster from them, all the customs, as well as all the signs of the old craftsmen, were conscientiously retained. To these individuals, the meaning now seemed to be missing, because the distinguished people who occupied themselves with it lacked the practical craftsman’s spirit <note>original: "Handwerks-Sinn" – the pragmatic, trade-oriented mindset of an actual builder</note>. When that strange thing, **Freemasonry** <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note>, was transplanted to France, Germany, and other countries where English customs were foreign, playful scholars or enthusiastic semi-intellectuals were unable to find any perspective from which to view this foreign phenomenon. As soon as one no longer knows what to think, a "miracle" is ready-made. Thus, people soon began to decipher the most hidden, incomprehensible mysteries of the spirit world and divine myths from the **square** <note>original: "Winkelmaaß"</note> and the **mason’s trowel**. But what the **Rosicrucians** <note>A mystical secret society that claimed to possess esoteric wisdom; here, the author mocks their complex interpretations</note> found in the mortar <note>original: "Kalk" – lime or mortar used in masonry</note> is truly the most absurd of all.

The old English masons called every place where they gathered for guild business a **Lodge** <note>original: "Loge"</note>, provided it was done according to the old customs of their **guild** <note>original: "Zunft"</note> and at least the minimum required number of members were present. This place might be in an inn or a barn, a peasant's hut, or even in an open field. The guild assembly itself *was* the Lodge. Its shape was an oblong rectangle, for that was how the assembly arranged itself. One

one of the oldest **guild-brothers** <note>original: "Zunft-Brüder" — members of the medieval stonemasons' corporations</note> would then usually draw the figure of an oblong rectangle with a piece of chalk on the table, the floor, or some board, so that everyone could see the proper, ancient positioning, and where the presiding Master, the **wardens** <note>original: "Worthalter" — literally "word-holders" or spokesmen of the guild</note>, and so on, were required to take their places.
