He asked: — “What is true brotherhood <note>original: 'Verbrüderung.' In this context, it refers to the spiritual and mystical bond between members of the secret society.</note>?”

They answered him: — “The connection of One, Three, Four, Five, Seven, and Nine.”

“What do these numbers mean?” — the Director asked.

The brothers answered:

“One signifies the beginning; Three signifies the Trinity <note>Dreyeinigkeit: While a Christian term, in Rosicrucian thought, it often refers to the three alchemical principles of Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt, or the threefold nature of man.</note>, from which have proceeded the Four active properties <note>Vier wirkenden Eigenschaften: These are likely the four classical elements—Fire, Air, Water, and Earth—which were believed to be the building blocks of the physical world.</note>, from which a Fifth Being <note>Fünftes Wesen: This refers to the 'quintessence' (literally the 'fifth essence'), a pure, celestial substance that was thought to permeate all things and hold the power of life.</note> arose, through which Wisdom has revealed itself in Seven <note>Seven: A significant number in Western esotericism, representing the seven classical planets and the seven metals of alchemy.</note>; and Nine is the end of all created things.”

Cedrinus understood no more from this explanation than from the previous one. But he was by now too accustomed to such experiences to find this striking. — The Director asked yet another question, which seemed to be only a repetition of the one immediately preceding it, but to which he nevertheless received a different answer.

“In what does true brotherhood consist?” — he asked.

And the response was — not “in the connection of One, Three, etc.,” as before, but:

“In an infinite circle, which contains within itself a terrifying power and an incomprehensible beauty.”

And the answer was: — “Through God and a good friend.”

“To what end have you become a brother?”

“To attain wisdom, art, and virtue, to please God and serve one's neighbor” — was the reply.

“By what means,” asked the Circle Director, “do you intend to prove that you are a brother?”

The answer was almost identical to those found in Masonic lodges <term>Masonic lodges: Local organizational units of Freemasonry. During the 18th century, many secret societies, including the Rosicrucians, borrowed heavily from Masonic rituals and terminology.</term>:

“Then let us,” said the Circle Director, “hear the touch and the word, by which we recognize one another as true brothers.”

Then the brothers formed a circle around the table, and the Master of Ceremonies <note>original: "Cerimoniarius." An officer responsible for overseeing the proper conduct of the order's rituals.</note>, who stood directly opposite the Circle Director, performed the touch <note>original: "Berührung." A specific, secret handshake used as a sign of recognition.</note> with the two brothers beside him, to the right and left, in return for which they gave him the word <note>original: "das Wort." A secret password, often of Hebrew or biblical origin, used to verify a member's rank and legitimacy.</note>. The ceremony was performed in the same order throughout the entire circle, until it reached the Circle Director, to whom the word was spoken aloud; whereupon he replied:

“Since you are all brothers; tell us: Where does wisdom have its seat?”

The brothers answered: — “In the center point of the light.” <note>original: "Mittelpunct des Lichts." In esoteric philosophy, this refers to a divine or spiritual source of enlightenment that the initiate seeks to reach.</note>

## Chapter Five.

### Progress in the Rosicrucian Mysteries.

---

Given his sound and healthy natural intellect, Cedrinus really should have had a correct idea of their true nature from that evening onward—having seen the <term>Rosicrucians<note>The Rosicrucians were a secretive spiritual and cultural movement that claimed to possess ancient esoteric wisdom.</note></term> in all their bare nakedness—had it not been for the fact that we ourselves, and the mood or direction of our <term>soul-powers<note>Original: *Seelenkräfte*. This term refers to the various faculties of the mind or psyche, such as reason, will, and emotion, which were thought to shape how a person perceives external reality.</note></term> through their activity in grasping the material provided to us by external objects, do more to form our perceptions than the objects themselves.

Had he not now tasted wisdom? And had he not, in the truest sense of the word, fallen <term>under the rod<note>Original: *unter den Stock gerathen*. This idiomatic expression refers to coming under strict disciplinary authority or punishment, likely reflecting the hierarchical and sometimes harsh nature of the secret society’s leadership.</note></term>? How was it possible for him still to expect anything from a Circle Director who treated people whom he called his brothers and sons of wis- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "Weis-" suggests the following word is "wisdom."</note>

<summary>This page concludes a Rosicrucian meeting and begins Chapter Five, which explores why Cedrinus remains loyal to the order despite witnessing its flaws. The narrator suggests that the internal state of a person's mind often outweighs the reality of external evidence.</summary>

<keywords>Rosicrucianism, Cedrinus, Circle Director, soul-powers, esoteric rituals, psychology of belief, secret societies</keywords>

--- Page 304 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the sentence begun on page 269, where the author was questioning how a "Circle Director" could treat his followers in such a way despite calling them brothers.</meta>

...dom <note>The word "wisdom" (Weisheit) is completed here from the previous page.</note>, in such a degrading manner, and yet with such seriousness and such earnest importance, could make them perform such — servile ceremonies? <note>Original: *hündische Ceremonien*. Literally "dog-like ceremonies," implying a humiliating, cringing, or fawning submissiveness demanded of the participants.</note> What could he expect from a man who so clearly delighted in playing the inspired despot and the ruling prophet? Whether weakness of the intellect or hypocrisy played the greater part in this pleasure and this game, it was nonetheless obvious that everything that had occurred was something he could not remember without blushing, were he to see it in its true form.

But how many mysteries had Cedrinus already participated in! No ceremonies were new to him anymore! And they occupied his imagination more than his intellect. And it was still devotion that guided the activity of his imagination. It was a devotion that was not so violent or wild as to produce fanatical <term>zealotry<note>Original: *Schwärmerey*. In the 18th century, this term referred to a state of irrational, excessive religious enthusiasm or mystical delusion, often viewed as a psychological ailment.</note></term>; yet it was far too passionate to reconcile itself with every deception that knew how to creep into his intellect in the garb of piety.

Cedrinus, in order to be healed, first had to experience the bitter fate of seeing the deception completely stripped bare, with himself standing beside it as a victim.

From his youth, Cedrinus had felt a kind of natural loathing toward <term>gold-making<note>Original: *Goldmacherey*. The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold; in this context, it likely refers to a physical object used in the ritual.</note></term> lay there within the entire mystery—Cedrinus still did not recognize it as gold-making!

When they were all assembled in the secret room, the <term>Circle Director<note>Original: *Cirkel-Director*. > “Nothing but secret friends and associates of the true brotherhood.”

“Then it is necessary,” said the man with the rod, “that the door be examined and that we place ourselves in complete security.”

The author refers to the mythical or legendary origins claimed by the Rosicrucians, often tracing their lineage back to ancient Egypt or the biblical patriarchs.</note>, and he gave each of the brothers an ascetic-theological text to work on, which was to be read aloud in the next **Convention** <term>Convention: A formal ritual assembly or business meeting of the lodge members.</term>. — For their reading, the Director recommended to them exclusively theosophical books: "The Compass of the Sages," "The Ring of Plato," **Jacob Böhme’s** writings, and the like. <note>original: "Compaß der Weisen" (1779) and "Annulus Platonis" (also known as the Aurea Catena Homeri). These were foundational texts for 18th-century alchemists and mystics.</note>

## Sixth Chapter.

### Steps Toward Discovery.

Under a man who possessed enough spiritual strength to rule with something better than the **Rod of Aaron** <note>A biblical reference to the staff of Moses' brother which symbolized divine authority; here, the author uses it to describe the leader's exercise of power.</note>, and who had been a **visionary** <note>original: "Schwärmer." A derogatory Enlightenment-era term for someone perceived as a religious fanatic or one prone to mystical delusions.</note> at the same time, **Cedrinus** might have been in real danger of becoming, for the rest of his life, a kind of quiet **theosopher** <note>A practitioner of theosophy, a system of belief that seeks direct knowledge of the divine through mystical insight and philosophical speculation.</note>, restricted more to his own inner world than acting upon the outside one. Among the tasks assigned for elaboration, the question: "Where is true rest of the soul to be found?" fell to his lot. He produced a composition on this subject, which I have here before me. I would like to call it a...

<meta>This page continues the psychological analysis of the character Cedrinus. The previous page ended with the suggestion that his early efforts in mystical oratory might be viewed in a specific light by a critical observer.</meta>

...might call it a **homily** <term>Homily: A religious discourse or sermon, usually focusing on spiritual or moral edification.</term> by a beginner, who worked out a piece of **oratory** <note>original: "redenden Kunst." Literally "the speaking art," referring to the formal discipline of rhetoric.</note> without being aware of the artistry in his own activity. There is much **imagination** and no small amount of effort in it. But from the whole, it is evident that the author himself did not recognize this effort as something learned, or as a technical skill. — A bit more vanity in his character, and a bit more insight and skill on the part of his leader: and **Cedrinus** would have been set on the straight path to becoming a **hypocrite** without even knowing it. There is no shorter way to this than the path of **rhetoric**. 

A successfully applied **talent**, crowned with ambiguous applause, which—in a precarious lack of awareness of its own art and unfamiliarity with its theory—ascribes the flattering glory to a truth other than the truth of art within the work of art, perhaps to an imagined **truth** in one's own fleeting sentiments (which are so easily mistaken for a permanent character), runs a great risk of falling into **delusions** <note>original: "Schwärmereyen." A common 18th-century term for religious fanaticism or "enthusiasm," often used by Enlightenment writers to critique those who claimed direct mystical inspiration.</note> that have no value at all in the eyes of a critical examiner. — Great and very famous people have left us warning examples in their histories. But to play the **orator**, **Cedrinus** was not vain enough, and he was no longer young enough to become an orator. He was not yet too old to become a **visionary** <note>original: "Schwärmer." In the 18th century, this term was used for those who believed they had direct, mystical communication with the divine—often used disparagingly by Enlightenment thinkers to mean a "fanatic."</note>; but his leader was too clumsy.

For about a year, he wavered back and forth between mystical exercises, confusing reading, and the effort to orient himself within the **circle-meetings** and the activities of the **Order**. Then Brother Minnas came again with the question, "whether he did not want to go further in the Order?" — **Cedrinus** agreed, in a kind of resignation to the judgment of the **Senior** <note>The "Senior" was a high-ranking officer within the secret society.</note> that was more good-natured and obliging than it was well-considered.

He paid about twelve **thalers** <term>Thaler: A large silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries; the word is the origin of the modern "dollar."</term> for admission into this second **Rosicrucian degree**, learned another **password** (it was: "**Water** <note>original: "Majim." The brothers were industriously examined here regarding the **state of their souls** and their knowledge of **Order affairs**; and, because precisely at that time in **Bavaria** the **Inquisition** <note>This refers to the historical suppression and investigation of secret societies, particularly the Illuminati, by the Bavarian Elector Karl Theodor beginning in 1784.</note> was being carried out against the **Illuminati**, **Fraxinus** warned them—with all the unction and gravity of a holder of the **Staff of Aaron** <note>The Staff of Aaron is a biblical symbol of divine authority and priestly legitimacy, used here by the lodge leader to emphasize his spiritual rank.</note>—to guard themselves against these dangerous people. But by now, Cedrinus had grown cold enough toward the "good cause" of spreading the Kingdom of Christ through the **Gold and Rosy Cross** <note>An 18th-century successor to the original Rosicrucian movement, heavily focused on alchemy and conservative Christian mysticism.</note> that he frankly confessed to the willing **Introductor** <note>The official responsible for bringing candidates into a new degree of the Order.</note> that he understood little of the instruction, and almost thought: "What little Hans doesn't learn, grown Hans never will." <note>A German proverb (original: "was Hänschen nicht lernt, wird Hans nimmermehr lernen") suggesting that if one does not grasp the basics early on, they never will.</note> He therefore wished to make no more vain attempts.

He also spoke very much in the tone of a man who makes a declaration rather than a proposal. — Half out of compliance, and half because of this tone of **Ninnas**, who treated the matter entirely as something that went without saying, Cedrinus gave his consent this time as well and went for the new admission on the appointed day.

## Chapter Seven.

### The Deception Ends.

Cedrinus had found sufficient information about the use of rainwater in Rosicrucian writings, especially in *The Golden Chain* <note>original: "Aurea catena". This refers to the *Aurea Catena Homeri* (The Golden Chain of Homer), a foundational 18th-century alchemical text that taught the unity of nature.</note> by the well-known Brother Homerus <note>Bruder Homerus: The pseudonym of Anton Joseph Kirchweger, an alchemist whose work was highly influential in Rosicrucian circles.</note>. He had learned there that through high, secret art, living animals—and in particular crayfish—could be prepared from putrefying rainwater. He was too good a connoisseur of crayfish, however, to believe in such a thing. He enjoyed eating them, but never once thought of creating them.

It was only natural that now, as he saw the "making" of metals and animals taking place almost before his very eyes, the whole indecent game finally became clear to him. This realization was, however, furthered by **Fraxinus’s** own carelessness.

**Fraxinus**, that great lover of the secret number three, sought to realize a kind of "Two-in-Unity" within his own person. He wished to wield the hammer of a **Provincial Grand Master** <term>Provincial Grand Master: A high-ranking administrative leader within a geographic region of Freemasonry.</term> of the **Freemasons** in one hand, and the **Rod of Aaron** <note>In the Bible, Aaron's rod performed miracles; here it symbolizes the magical and spiritual authority claimed by the Rosicrucian leaders.</note> in the other. The Freemasons

<summary>Cedrinus begins to see through the alchemical frauds of his order, specifically the claim that life can be spontaneously generated from rainwater, while the leader Fraxinus attempts to consolidate Masonic and Rosicrucian power.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Rosicrucianism, spontaneous generation, Fraxinus, Freemasonry, Aurea Catena Homeri, skepticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the manipulation of Masonic lodges by the Rosicrucian leader Fraxinus. It follows the previous page's discussion of Cedrinus’s growing awareness of the order's deceptions.</meta>

did not know that he was a Rosicrucian. Only a few suspected it, and these individuals were made objects of suspicion. His plan, however, was to shepherd the Freemasons as well with that rod and staff <note>A biblical reference to Psalm 23, used here to imply Fraxinus’s desire to exert spiritual and administrative control over the Masonic lodges.</note>. They were intended to serve as a training ground for the most holy Order of the **Gold and Rosy Cross** <term>Gold and Rosy Cross: A highly secretive 18th-century German Masonic-Rosicrucian order that claimed to possess ancient knowledge of alchemy and magic.</term>. He therefore sought to insert all so-called grand officials from among the companions of his secret order into the Freemason lodges.

In the year 1784—at a time when **Cedrinus** wore only the apron of the **Theoretical Brothers of Solomonic Wisdom** <term>Theoretical Brothers: The entry-level degree of the Gold and Rosy Cross, where members studied the "theory" of nature and alchemy before beginning laboratory work.</term>—the Grand Keeper of the Seal and Archives of the four united lodges (which, without knowing it, had fallen under **Aaron's Rod** <note>original: "Aarons Stecken". Symbolizing the magical authority claimed by the Rosicrucian leaders.</note>) left the Order. **Fraxinus** then cast his eyes upon **Cedrinus**, intending to fill the vacant position with someone from the innermost sanctuary. The meritorious **Minnas**, who just shortly before had the kindness to persuade **Cedrinus** toward actual Rosicrucianism, was sent out to induce him to accept this honorary post. **Fraxinus** was never the man to let an order perish so long as he could save it. Therefore, he also firmly insisted that every grand official of the Freemasons be an **Old Scot** <term>Old Scot: Refers to "Scottish" degrees in Freemasonry, which in this period often served as high-ranking "inner" circles that the Rosicrucians infiltrated to control the lower lodges.</term>. They paid one hundred imperial—

**Cedrinus** did so. In November of the year 1784, he became an **Old Scot** and **Grand Keeper of the Seal** <term>Grand Keeper of the Seal: An official responsible for the lodge's archives and the authenticating stamps used on formal documents.</term>.

But now that he had the archives under his supervision—where the records of all Masonic history in which the superiors of the **Hamburg Lodges** had taken part were kept—**Cedrinus** saw how orders are manufactured and how secret societies are propagated.

There is no disposition toward any kind of **duality** <note>original: "Zweyeinigkeit". Here used to mean double-dealing or split loyalty; the author suggests Cedrinus was too straightforward and honest to play a double game.</note> in the character of **Cedrinus**. In these secret societies, members were often given "Order names" derived from Latin or Greek to hide their true identities.</note> to turn the **Freemason Lodges** into a nursery for the **Rosicrucians**, even though he himself was a **Rosicrucian**.

**Fraxinus** possessed the heroic persistence to pursue those attempts with the **Freemasons** with all the more zeal, the more unsuccessfully the affairs of the **Secret Superiors** <term>Secret Superiors: (Geheime Oberen) The alleged hidden leaders of high-degree Masonic and Rosicrucian systems to whom members owed absolute obedience, often claiming ancient or supernatural origins.</term> were proceeding within the **Rosicrucian Conventions** themselves. Even when *Aneopes*, *Cedrinus*, and *Trimarchus* had already shared with one another their suspicions regarding **gold-making** <note>Alchemical gold-making was a core promise of the "Gold and Rosy Cross" order, used to attract members; by the 1780s, many members were becoming skeptical when no gold was produced.</note>, their fear of the hidden superiors, and had demanded that their identities be made known; even when **Fraxinus** himself had already delivered rebukes and warnings in the conventions because of this, he still sought to push through his plans in the **Freemason Lodges**. Even after he had already declared in a **Rosicrucian Convention** that he no longer wished to be **Circle Director** <term>Circle Director: (Cirkel-Director) A high-ranking administrative officer in the Rosicrucian hierarchy responsible for overseeing a specific geographical group or "circle."</term>, he employed cunning, a defiant reliance on his authority, and other very inadequate shifts to push through and validate laws that would have made the **Freemason Lodges** entirely dependent on associates of orders kept secret from the **Freemasons**.
